## 5 Torque-limiting gear with flexing spring arms (compliant slip hub)

*torque limiting & slip.* A one-piece plastic gear or hub whose built-in springy arms grip the shaft and let it slip when overloaded, with no separate parts

*Also covers:* Also covers the RC "servo saver" (a sprung servo horn that gives way under shock)

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A plastic gear or hub with springy arms built into it that hug the shaft tightly enough to drive it, but let the gear slip round when something jams; the RC 'servo saver' is a spring-loaded servo arm that gives way when the wheels are knocked and then springs back
- **term**: Compliant torque-limiting gear (compliant slip hub, flexure slip clutch)
- **aliases**: 
  - compliant slip hub
  - flexure slip clutch
  - torque limiting gear (DuPont design guide term)
  - gear with sliding coupling / split-hub coupling (DuPont design guide term)
  - slip gear
  - slipping gear
  - clutch gear
  - friction hub
  - spring-arm clutch
  - friction-type torque limiter with a split resin sleeve (printer paper-feed parts)
  - servo saver (RC cars: a sprung or flexible servo arm)
  - adjustable servo saver
  - flexible servo arm / flexible servo horn *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **family**: torque limiting & slip
- **other_families**: compliant mechanisms & flexures, print-in-place & 3D-printing joints, clutches & couplings
- **one_liner**: A gear or hub whose own flexible arms grip the shaft by spring force, so it drives normally but slips instead of stripping teeth when overloaded, with no separate springs, balls or nuts.
- **complexity**: Single piece: a gear or hub with built-in spring arms (split hub fingers or curved flexures), plus the shaft or inner ring it grips; long-life versions add one part, a metal ring spring or O-ring around the split hub. The servo saver is a few parts: an inner hub on the servo spline, an outer arm, a spring (coil or torsion spring, or the flexible plastic itself), a sleeve and a collar or nut
- **how_it_works**: The hub is split into several springy arms or fingers that are made slightly too small for the shaft (or an inner ring), so they have to bend outwards to fit; their spring-back presses on the shaft, and friction carries the turning force. When the load needs more torque than that grip can carry, the arms slide round the shaft (or flex enough to hop out of small notches) and the gear slips, then grips again once the overload ends. A servo saver does the same job on a servo's arm: the two halves of the arm are held in line by a preloaded spring or by flexible plastic, so a knock on the wheels makes the arm give instead of the servo's gears, and the spring pulls it back to centre.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: slip or let go when overloaded: protects plastic gear teeth, small motors and servos, join and hold: it also holds the gear on its shaft, with no key or set screw
- **motion**: rotate: 1:1 rotation while gripping; unlimited slipping rotation when overloaded. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 while gripping (gear and shaft turn together); 1 while slipping (the gear turns relative to the shaft). Servo saver: 1 (the arm can turn a little relative to the servo shaft against a spring)
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - torque-limiting: slips (or gives, for a servo saver) at a torque set by the stiffness and pre-bend of its arms
  - self-aligning or self-centring: the servo saver's spring pulls the arm back into line after a knock, so steering returns to centre
  - Resets itself: nothing to replace after an overload, unlike a shear pin
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): the arms press on the shaft and friction carries the torque
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): the arms are the spring; a servo saver uses a coil or torsion spring or flexible TPU *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **input_output**: continuous rotation -> the same rotation, ratio 1:1, until the slip torque; then the output stops or slows while the input keeps turning. Efficiency close to 100% while gripping; slipping power becomes heat. Gear or hub mounted on a shaft (shafts in line or the gear meshing with parallel shafts). Back-drivable: yes, it slips at roughly the same torque in both directions. Servo saver: servo arm angle -> steering link angle 1:1, with springy give under shock
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: the gear or hub pushes or snaps onto the shaft and can be pulled off; a servo saver screws onto the servo's splined output
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: it can slip, and be fitted and removed, many times, but plastic arms slowly relax and wear so the slip torque falls; printed ones are cheap to reprint
- **tools_needed**: none for most (push on by hand; a vice or small press for tight fits); a screwdriver for the screw that holds a servo saver on the servo; a spanner for an adjustable servo saver's collar or nut
- **seal**: none
- **load**: light (to medium for larger moulded gears): suited to small instrument, dial, toy and printer drives and RC steering. DuPont suggests torque-limiting gears for plastic gear drives in meat grinders, can openers and hand drill presses, and says split-hub couplings are fine where the required torque is very low. Strong only in twist up to the slip torque; side loads must be carried by the shaft and its bearings
- **adjustable**: mostly no: the slip torque is fixed by the arm thickness, length, material and how much they are pre-bent; to change it, reprint with a different fit or height, or add an O-ring or metal ring spring around a split hub. Some servo savers have a threaded collar that sets the spring preload (tamiyamodel.com: adjustable types let you adjust the spring tension)
- **play_and_precision**: Little or no play while gripping (DuPont even uses springy flexing gear parts to remove backlash). After it slips the output loses its angular position, so re-home anything with a position sensor on the motor side. A servo saver adds some play (steering 'slop'), and some models add a slight delay in steering response (tamiyamodel.com). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: it slides or snaps onto the shaft end
- **materials**: Moulded: acetal (POM, e.g. Delrin (brand)) gears on nylon shafts (DuPont examples); a resin sleeve with axial slits squeezed by a metal ring spring (printer torque limiter patent). 3D printed: PETG (flexes and tolerates heat, preferred by Printables designers), nylon, ABS, or TPU for flexible servo arms; avoid PLA for flexing arms because it is brittle and relaxes under constant load. Servo savers: plastic or aluminium arms with a steel coil spring, metal sleeve and nut. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: it prints in one piece with no extra hardware, but the grip depends on a tiny amount of interference (a fraction of a millimetre) and on the material, so expect several test prints and tuning; ready-made RC servo savers are easy to buy and fit

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): PETG, nylon, ABS or TPU gears, hubs and flexible servo arms
  - injection moulding (industrial): acetal and nylon gears with moulded-in springs (DuPont)
  - CNC: flexural elements machined into a gear's hub (Slocum)
  - off-the-shelf part: RC servo savers, printer-type friction torque limiters, toy-kit clutch gears *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: yes: the gear or hub with its spring arms prints as one finished, working piece that simply pushes onto a shaft. Long-life designs add a separate metal ring spring or O-ring around the split hub, which makes them 'partly'
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Print the gear flat on the bed so the spring arms lie in the layer plane and bend within the layers; do not build flexing arms vertically in Z, because layer bonds are weak (Hubs snap-fit guide). Make each arm at least 1 mm thick at its root and add a fillet of at least half the arm thickness where it joins the hub (Hubs). Use PETG, nylon, ABS or TPU rather than PLA: the Printables RC slipping-gear designer prints the clutch part in PETG because it 'needs to flex and accept some heat', while PLA is fine for the other parts; Make the grip parametric (interference and hub height) and print two or three versions: the Printables designer's first version would not slip at all, and he then offered a 'slips quite easily' part and a stronger part. Test the slip with a drill on its lowest torque setting holding the gear, as that designer suggests. Do not let the arms stay bent when not needed: DuPont's torque-limiting gear uses three pivoting springs so they do not remain loaded by accident, and plastic springs lose force under constant load (stress relaxation), so for a steady torque over a long time add a metal ring spring around the hub (DuPont). For a flexible servo arm, the Printables TPU servo saver was printed at 0.1 mm layers, 230 °C nozzle, 60 °C bed, 20 mm/s, retraction off, in two stiffnesses. Slipping makes heat; PLA softens above about 60 °C (Prusa) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Servo saver (RC car steering), adjustable servo saver, aluminium servo saver, servo saver spring; servo horn (servo arm) for the servo's spline; friction-type torque limiter for office equipment (split resin sleeve with coil or C-ring spring, as in US patent application US20030050121A1); toy-kit clutch gears (e.g. LEGO Technic (brand) 24-tooth clutch gear); O-rings or spring-steel C-rings to add grip around a split hub; tolerance rings (a metal alternative)
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Torque-limiting devices are very useful on plastic gears to stop tooth damage on overload, e.g. in meat grinders, can openers and hand drill presses; the springs must not remain accidentally in the loaded position, which DuPont's example achieves with three pivoting springs (DuPont Design Guide Module I, ch. 8 'Gears', pp. 47-48, Fig. 8.05)
  - A split hub used as a slipping coupling is fine where the torque is very low (e.g. a small dial setting device); if a constant torque must be carried for a long time, add a metal spring around the hub to keep the force constant (DuPont Module I, p. 48, Fig. 8.06)
  - Plastic interference fits on shafts only suit low torque, because stress relaxation of the plastic can let them slip over time (DuPont Module I, p. 63): design the slip torque with margin and expect it to fall
  - Machine or mould flexural elements into the gear's hub to keep a steady preload between gear and shaft, rather than relying on a plain press fit as the clutch (Slocum, FUNdaMENTALs of Design, Topic 5, 'Wheels: Clutches & Differentials')
  - Flexing arms for FDM: root thickness at least 1 mm, root fillet radius at least 0.5 x the thickness, never build them vertically in Z; prefer ABS, nylon or TPU (Hubs, 'How to design snap-fit joints for 3D printing') *(unverified parts omitted)*

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - The servo saver on the steering of a hobby RC car, which protects the steering servo when the front wheels hit something (tamiyamodel.com)
  - Toy gearboxes with a small clutch inside a gear, e.g. a push-along toy dog whose broken internal clutch stopped it gripping (Printables 'replacement gear with clutch for toy')
  - Printer paper-feed rollers that use a one-piece resin torque limiter (split sleeve with a ring spring) (US patent application US20030050121A1)
  - Dial and clock setting knobs that turn with a light, slipping grip (DuPont Fig. 8.06)
- **when_to_use**: When you want simple, built-in overload protection in a printed or moulded part with no extra hardware: toy and hobby gear drives, small robot grippers, pan-tilt heads and end-stop protection for small geared motors, settable dials and knobs, and servo protection in RC cars and small robots. Good when part count, size and cost matter more than an exact or adjustable slip torque
- **when_not_to_use**: Not for high or precisely known torques, or where the torque must stay the same for years under constant load, because plastic arms relax and wear (use an adjustable friction slip clutch with a metal spring, or a ball-detent torque limiter). Not where the part must hold its position or hold a load against gravity. Not where it may slip for long periods or in hot places (plastic softens). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **environment_limits**: Plastic springs lose force over time under constant load (stress relaxation), faster when warm, so the slip torque drops (DuPont Module I, pp. 47-48 and 63). PLA softens and deforms above about 60 °C (Prusa PLA guide); PETG is rated for use below about 80 °C (Prusa PETG guide). Heat from slipping adds to this. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Low risk. It limits torque but does not make a mechanism finger-safe. A printed arm can crack and fall into a gearbox. None known beyond the usual pinch points of gears and steering linkages
- **common_failures**: Too tight: it never slips and the teeth strip anyway (the Printables RC slipping-gear designer's first version would not slip). Too loose, or loosened over time by stress relaxation: it slips in normal use (DuPont). Arms crack at the root if they are thin, have no fillet, are printed vertically or are made of brittle PLA (Hubs). Long slipping heats and wears the hub and shaft. Printed servo savers can be weaker than the moulded originals and break often (Printables HPI servo saver replacement). Servo saver too soft: vague steering ('slop'). Avoid by tuning with test prints, using PETG, nylon or TPU, adding fillets, and adding a metal ring spring where the torque must stay steady
- **related**: 
  - Friction slip clutch (slip coupling, slipper clutch): the same slip-by-friction idea with a separate metal spring and nut, so it is adjustable and steadier, but has more parts
  - Ratchet-tooth torque limiter: sloped teeth on springy arms click over each other at a set torque; one-direction, noisy
  - Ball-detent torque limiter: spring-loaded balls pop out of dimples; more precise and re-engages at the same angle
  - Tolerance ring (wavy-band press fit): a metal wavy band does the same gripping-and-slipping job between a shaft and a hole, with less relaxation than plastic
  - Servo horn and spline hub: the plain, rigid way to attach an arm to a servo, with no protection
  - Series-elastic coupling: a deliberate spring between motor and output that gives under shock but does not slip
  - Compliant ratchet and overrunning clutch (flexure pawl): printed spring arms that give one-way motion instead of torque limiting
  - Shear-pin coupling (breakaway link): breaks instead of slipping; some printed servo savers are simply designed to break at a set point

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [General Design Principles for DuPont Engineering Polymers (Design Guide Module I), ch. 8 'Gears', p. 48](https://www.distrupol.com/General_Design_Principles_for_Engineering_Polymers.pdf): Line drawings Fig. 8.05 'Torque limiting gear' (gear with pivoting moulded-in springs) and Fig. 8.06 'Gear with sliding coupling' (split hub acting as a slipping coupling), plus Fig. 8.02 hub-to-rim flexible web *(licence: link only (© 2000 DuPont; cite facts in our own words))*

  - [Safety clutch 14 (Nguyen Duc Thang, YouTube thang010146)](https://youtu.be/Apye3XXRpYU): CAD animation: two parts of an arm held in line by a leaf spring that bends and lets them rotate apart when the machine crashes, the same idea as an RC servo saver *(licence: link only (YouTube, all rights reserved))*
  - [US20030050121A1 'Torque limiter and rotary member with the torque limiter' (Google Patents)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US20030050121A1/en): Patent drawings of a one-piece resin gear boss with a slit sleeve squeezed onto a shaft by a ring spring, used in printer paper feeds *(licence: link only (patent document))*
- **make_it_yourself**: 
  - [Orlandoo Hunter OH32X02 Servo Saver (Aspengt, Printables model id 383115)](https://api.printables.com/graphql/): Replacement servo arm for a micro RC truck printed in TPU so the arm itself flexes and acts as a servo saver, in two stiffnesses (read through the Printables public API; the web page could not be opened here) *(licence: CC BY-NC-SA)*

## 6 Shear-pin coupling (breakaway link)

*torque limiting & slip.* A deliberately weak pin or link that snaps at a set load, sacrificing itself to protect the rest of the machine; replaced after it breaks

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A deliberately weak pin or thin link that carries the drive normally but snaps cleanly when something jams, so the cheap pin breaks instead of the expensive gears; afterwards you fit a new pin
- **term**: Shear-pin coupling (shear-pin torque limiter, mechanical fuse)
- **aliases**: 
  - shear pin
  - shear bolt (snowblower augers)
  - shear-pin coupling
  - shear-pin hub
  - shear-pin sprocket
  - roller chain shear pin coupling
  - breakaway link
  - weak link
  - mechanical fuse
  - sacrificial pin (sacrificial part)
  - fuse pin (aircraft engine mountings)
  - nylon shear pin / shear screw (model rocketry)
  - safety pin (as some snowblower owners call the auger shear bolt)
  - frangible link (a related one-shot breaking part)
- **family**: torque limiting & slip
- **other_families**: clutches & couplings
- **one_liner**: A pin, bolt or thin section sized to break at a set force or torque, so it disconnects the drive (or lets a joint go) before anything more valuable is damaged, and is replaced afterwards, like an electrical fuse.
- **complexity**: A few parts: a hub or collar and a shaft (or two coupling halves or flanges) with holes that line up, the shear pin or shear bolt (often with a narrowed neck or groove where it should break), and a keeper such as a split (cotter) pin, nut or circlip. A breakaway link can also be a single printed or moulded part with a deliberately thin neck
- **how_it_works**: The pin passes through both parts, for example a propeller hub and its shaft, so it locks them together by shape and carries all the turning force across one or two cut planes (it is loaded in shear). Its diameter, neck and material are chosen so it breaks at a known force that is lower than what the gears, shaft or motor can take. When a jam or impact exceeds that force the pin cuts cleanly, the two parts are free to turn separately (an outboard engine races with no thrust), and the rest of the machine is saved; the broken pieces are knocked out and a new pin is fitted. The same trick is used to hold something until a set force is reached, such as nylon screws holding a rocket's nose cone on until the ejection charge fires, or the plastic seal on a fire extinguisher's pull pin.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - slip or let go when overloaded: it breaks and disconnects the drive at a set torque or force (one time only)
  - join and hold: until it breaks it is simply a solid pin joint (it can also be used to hold parts together until a set force releases them)
- **motion**: none: a rigid joint until the pin breaks; afterwards the parts rotate (or separate) freely until a new pin is fitted
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 0 while intact (the hub and shaft are locked together); after breaking, 1 for a rotating coupling (free rotation) or the parts come apart completely for a straight-pull breakaway link
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - fail-safe or breakaway: breaks at a set load to protect the rest of the machine, like an electrical fuse
  - torque-limiting: one-shot, and it disconnects completely rather than slipping (Wikipedia lists it as a 'disconnect type' torque limiter)
- **holding_principle**: shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the pin through both parts carries the load
- **input_output**: continuous rotation -> the same rotation, ratio 1:1, with no slip and close to 100% efficiency until the pin breaks; afterwards no drive at all (little or no torque reaches the load). Shafts in line (shear-pin coupling) or a hub on a shaft (sprocket, propeller, auger, pulley). Back-drivable: yes while intact (it is a rigid joint)
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: the pin is held by a split pin, nut or circlip and is knocked out and replaced; the pin itself is single-use
- **reuse_cycles**: once: each pin works only once and must be replaced after it breaks (the hub or coupling around it is reused many times)
- **tools_needed**: Pliers for the split pin, a spanner for the prop or bolt nut, and a hammer and punch to drive out the broken pieces (JLM Marine; YouRepair); a drill (and sometimes a tap) to make the cross hole; a thread tap for screw-in nylon rocket shear pins in hard tubes (Apogee)
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light to heavy depending on the pin: one 2-56 nylon rocketry pin breaks at roughly 21 lb (about 95 N) (Apogee: three pins take 64.24 lb); industrial roller-chain shear pin couplings are rated from 738 to 66,504 in-lb (about 83 to 7,500 N·m) (Dalton). Strong in twist up to its breaking torque; it is loaded in shear, and the hub and shaft carry any bending or side loads
- **adjustable**: no: to change the breaking load you fit a different pin (diameter, neck size, material) or a different number of pins
- **play_and_precision**: Little or no play if the pin fits its holes closely; it keeps the exact angular position until it breaks. The breaking torque can be quite accurate when the neck diameter and material are closely controlled (Dalton), but fatigue, wear, corrosion and material batch all shift it. It tolerates no misalignment, since it is a rigid joint
- **access_needed**: both sides: the pin passes right through the hub and shaft and the broken pieces have to be driven out from one side to the other (screw-in nylon rocket pins need only one side)
- **materials**: Pins: metals chosen and tempered to be brittle (so they snap rather than bend) and resistant to fatigue (Wikipedia 'Shear pin'), such as brass (a 6 mm brass pin in a stand-mixer pasta-roller attachment, per a Printables repair model), stainless steel (316 for salt water, JLM Marine), specified low-strength bolts (snowblowers), and nylon screws (rocketry). Hubs and coupling halves are made harder than the pin; Dalton hardens its coupling halves to 45-50 HRC. Printed breakaway links: PLA snaps cleanly but softens with heat; *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to make (drill a cross hole and fit a softer pin, or print a part with a thin neck) but hard to calibrate: the breaking load of small pins and printed parts varies, so make spares and test-break several

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: shear pins and shear bolts made for the machine, outboard propeller shear pins, nylon shear screws, shear-pin hubs and sprockets, shear-pin couplings
  - drilling (metal or woodwork): cross-drill the hub and shaft together so the holes line up
  - CNC or lathe: pins with an accurately turned neck
  - 3D print (FDM): printed weak links, breakaway arms and test pins *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the pin is a separate, replaceable part by design. A one-piece printed breakaway link (a part with a deliberately thin neck) is possible, but then the whole part is reprinted after it breaks
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: A printed part can be the weak link, e.g. a printed PLA servo saver made at 100% infill that is designed to break at a marked point on impact and was tested to do so (Printables servo saver for a DT-03 buggy), or a printed pin through a metal hub. Print orientation decides where it breaks: layers are the weakest direction, so either put the intended break across the layers deliberately, or print pins lying down and put the weak neck in the geometry. In a shear test of upright-printed 10 mm PLA pins, pins built from concentric perimeters broke predictably at about 46 MPa shear stress, while zig-zag infill gave random failures (Birosz et al., Polymers 2022), so use concentric infill or all perimeters for repeatable pins. Keep the same material, printer and settings for every copy, test-break at least a few, and keep printed spares. PLA softens above about 60 °C (Prusa) and creeps under steady load, so a PLA link can break early in a hot place or after long loading. Do not swap a machine's specified metal shear pin for a printed one on powered equipment (a Printables snowblower shear bolt printed in PLA exists) unless you have tested that it breaks at the right load, because a pin that is too weak breaks in normal use and one that is too strong protects nothing
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Shear pin, shear bolt (snowblower auger shear bolts, commonly 1/4 in or 5/16 in, per YouRepair), outboard propeller shear pin with split (cotter) pin, shear-pin hub, shear-pin sprocket, roller chain shear pin coupling, nylon shear pins (2-56 nylon machine screws for rocketry), split pins, nylon-insert lock nuts; replacement shear pins are sold for each shear-pin hub size
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Average shear stress in a pin cut on two planes (double shear) is F / (2 x pi x d^2 / 4) (Engineers Edge, 'Bolt or Pin in Double Shear'); so a pin through a shaft of radius r breaks at roughly torque = 2 x (pi x d^2 / 4) x (shear strength of the pin material) x r (derived from that formula)
  - An accurate release torque comes from precision machining of the pin's neck diameter and from the pin material; the coupling halves are hardened (45-50 HRC) so only the pin breaks (Dalton Gear, roller chain shear pin coupling)
  - Choose and temper the pin so it is brittle (snaps rather than bends) and resists fatigue, otherwise it may break early in normal running (Wikipedia 'Shear pin')
  - Never replace a shear pin or shear bolt with a stronger pin or ordinary hardened bolt; use the part the manufacturer specifies (JLM Marine; YouRepair)
  - Three 2-56 nylon shear pins need about 64 lb (about 285 N) in total to shear, i.e. roughly 21 lb (95 N) each; they are consumable and replaced after every flight (Apogee Components)
  - Upright-printed 10 mm PLA pins sheared at about 46 MPa; concentric infill gave more predictable failure than zig-zag infill (Birosz, Andó and Safranyik, 'Layer Adhesion Test of Additively Manufactured Pins: A Shear Test', Polymers 14(1):55, 2022)

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - The shear bolts on a snowblower's auger, which snap if it hits a rock or ice chunk (Wikipedia 'Shear pin'; YouRepair)
  - The shear pin behind an outboard motor's propeller: hit something and the engine revs with no thrust (JLM Marine)
  - The brass shear pin inside a stand-mixer pasta-roller attachment that breaks if the rollers jam (Printables repair model)
  - The plastic seal that holds a fire extinguisher's pull pin until you tug it (Wikipedia 'Shear pin'), and the nylon screws that hold a model rocket's nose cone on until the ejection charge fires (Apogee)
- **when_to_use**: When overloads are rare but would be expensive (a rock in an auger, a propeller strike, a robot arm crash), when you want the drive fully disconnected on overload, and when you need a cheap, compact, very simple protector that transmits the normal load with no slip or backlash. Also when something must hold firmly until a clear force is reached and then let go completely: rocket nose cones, tamper seals, breakaway mounts, and printed weak links that protect an expensive servo or gearbox
- **when_not_to_use**: Not when overloads happen often, because every trip means stopping and fitting a new pin (use a friction slip clutch or a resettable ball-detent torque limiter). Not in a lifting or load-holding path where losing the drive would let a load fall or run back, unless that is safe. Not with vibrating or repeatedly reversing loads near the limit, which fatigue the pin so it breaks early. Not where an operator is likely to 'fix' repeated breaking by fitting a stronger bolt
- **environment_limits**: Fatigue from repeated loads lowers the breaking force, which is why pins are made fatigue-resistant (Wikipedia 'Shear pin'). Corrosion can weaken a pin or seize it in its hole; stainless (316) pins and marine grease are used on boats (JLM Marine). Printed PLA links soften above about 60 °C (Prusa) and creep under steady load. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: When the pin breaks the drive suddenly runs free: the motor races, and anything it was holding can drop or run back. Stop the engine or motor and remove the spark plug lead or unplug it before replacing a pin (JLM Marine; YouRepair). Never fit a stronger bolt in place of a shear pin: it removes the protection and can wreck the gearbox or bend the auger (YouRepair; JLM Marine). Rocketry shear pins are part of a pyrotechnic system; follow rocketry safety rules *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: Never breaks because a stronger bolt was fitted, so the gearbox or shaft is damaged instead (YouRepair; JLM Marine). Bends instead of snapping if the pin is too ductile, which can jam the hub (Wikipedia on brittleness). Printed pins vary with print settings and orientation (Birosz et al.). Avoid these by using the specified pin, keeping spares, greasing against corrosion, and testing printed links *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Friction slip clutch (slip coupling, slipper clutch): slips and keeps going, then resets by itself; nothing to replace, but less exact and gets hot if it slips for long
  - Ball-detent torque limiter: disconnects or clicks at a set torque and resets itself, so it suits frequent overloads, but it costs more and is bigger
  - Torque-limiting gear with flexing spring arms (compliant slip hub) and the RC servo saver: plastic springs that give or slip instead of breaking
  - Magnetic coupling: slips with no contact and resets automatically; lower torque
  - Tamper-evident breakaway bridge (frangible link): thin bridges that break the first time a cap is opened, to show it has been opened rather than to protect a machine
  - Breakaway clasp (e.g. a printed safety lanyard clasp that pulls apart at about 20 lb and clips back together): a reusable, snap-apart cousin

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [Shear pin (Wikipedia)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shear_pin): Plain explanation of shear pins as mechanical fuses and as 'hold until a set force' devices (snowblowers, propellers, aircraft fuse pins, fire extinguishers), with a photo of a sailor measuring a shear pin *(licence: text CC BY-SA 4.0 (reuse with credit); photo public domain)*
  - [US Navy photo: machinery repairman checks the outside diameter of a shear pin (Wikimedia Commons)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:US_Navy_110406-N-OY799-152_Machinery_Repairman_3rd_Class_Heather_Fogle,_from_Midlothian,_Texas,_checks_the_outside_diameter_of_a_shear_pin_in_the_m.jpg): Photo of a machined shear pin being measured with a micrometer in a ship's machine shop *(licence: public domain (US Navy work))*
  - [Shear pin, Unit 24, Gorge Powerhouse (Historic American Engineering Record photo, Wikimedia Commons)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:SHEAR_PIN,_UNIT_24_GORGE_POWERHOUSE._THE_WICKET_GATES_ON_THE_TURBINE_ARE_EACH_EQUIPPED_WITH_A_SHEAR_PIN_AND_OIL_PRESSURE_GAUGE._IF_A_GATE_JAMS,_THE_PIN_SMEARS_AND_THE_CHANGE_HAER_WASH,37-NEHA.V,1-B-18.tif): Photo of a shear pin on a hydro turbine's wicket-gate linkage; each gate has a shear pin so a jammed gate breaks its pin instead of the linkage *(licence: public domain (US National Park Service / HAER))*
  - [Safety clutch 1 (Nguyen Duc Thang, YouTube thang010146)](https://youtu.be/IUZAmjjQ7MA): CAD animation: a toggle-operated blade shears a soft pin so that clamping jaws open and release an excessive load *(licence: link only (YouTube, all rights reserved))*
  - [Safety clutch 2 (Nguyen Duc Thang, YouTube thang010146)](https://youtu.be/trfFKC7xnTw): CAD animation: two bars fixed together by a bolt; when the slider crashes the bolt breaks and the bars become a free pivot, protecting the other parts *(licence: link only (YouTube, all rights reserved))*
- **make_it_yourself**: 
  - [Servo saver for Tamiya Neo Fighter DT 03 Buggy (DT07, Printables model id 858648)](https://api.printables.com/graphql/): Printable one-piece PLA servo saver (100% infill) designed to break at marked points on impact, tested by the designer; a printed breakaway link protecting a servo (read through the Printables public API; the web page could not be opened here) *(licence: CC BY-NC)*
  - [Kitchenaid Pasta Roller shear pin replacement (Flex, Printables model id 1298480)](https://api.printables.com/graphql/): Repair model for a stand-mixer pasta-roller attachment, whose 6 mm brass shear pin breaks before the mixer does if the rollers jam; explains how the original pin works and a bolt-based repair (read through the Printables public API) *(licence: CC BY-NC-SA)*
  - [Small Nylon Shear Pins, 20 pack (Apogee Components)](https://www.apogeerockets.com/Building-Supplies/Misc-Hardware/Nylon-Shear-Pins-20-pack): Ready-made 2-56 x 1/4 in nylon screws used as shear pins, with step-by-step fitting instructions and the force to shear three pins; a cheap way to try shear pins *(licence: link only (product page))*
- **diagram_idea**: Side cut-away of a gear hub on a shaft joined by one cross pin with a narrowed neck at the shaft surface, animated so the drive turns normally, then the output hits a rock, the pin snaps at the neck, the shaft spins freely inside the stopped hub, and a new pin is tapped in.

## 7 Ratchet and pawl (incl. reversible ratchet)

*one-way & ratchet.* A toothed wheel and a hinged catch (pawl) that lets the wheel turn one way with a click and stops it turning back; a switchable pawl reverses the direction

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A wheel with saw-like teeth and a hinged catch that clicks over the teeth when the wheel turns one way and jams against them when it tries to turn back, so the wheel only turns one way; flip or switch the catch and it works the other way round
- **term**: Ratchet and pawl (ratchet wheel and pawl, ratchet gearing); called a reversible (reversing) ratchet when the pawl can be switched to lock the other direction
- **aliases**: 
  - ratchet
  - ratchet wheel and pawl / pawl and ratchet
  - ratchet gear / ratchet gearing (Machinery's Handbook)
  - click and ratchet wheel ('click' is an old word for the pawl; Durley: 'a ratchet or click (sometimes called a pawl)')
  - detent (Machinery's Handbook: 'a pawl or detent')
  - running ratchet (turns one way only) vs stationary or locking ratchet (pawl stops both directions), Reuleaux's terms used by Durley
  - reversible ratchet / reversing ratchet / bi-directional ratchet
  - double-pawl or dual-pawl ratchet; fine-tooth ratchet (socket wrenches)
  - ratchet feed (a pawl on a rocking lever that steps the wheel round)
  - pawl freewheel (the usual bicycle freewheel: spring-loaded pawls in a toothed drum)
  - Sperrklinke (German for the pawl, from the Wikimedia Commons file 'Sperrklinke Schema.svg')
  - cliquet (French, from the Commons file 'Roue libre cliquet.svg')
  - 棘轮机构 (Chinese, ratchet mechanism; 双向棘轮 = reversible ratchet, from the HUANGYAN920 mechanism library)
- **family**: one-way & ratchet
- **other_families**: indexing & detents, motion conversion, self-locking & anti-back-drive, tensioning, reels & take-up
- **one_liner**: A toothed wheel with a spring-held catch (pawl) that lets the wheel turn freely one way with a click and locks it against turning back, used to hold winches and reels, to step a wheel round one tooth at a time, and, with a switchable pawl, in reversible tools such as socket wrenches.
- **complexity**: A few parts: a ratchet wheel (a disc with lopsided, saw-like teeth), a pawl (a pivoting catch), a pivot pin or screw, something to keep the pawl pressed in (a small spring, the pawl's own weight, or a pawl that is itself springy) and a frame. A reversible ratchet adds either a double-ended pawl or two pawls plus a selector switch or lever. A ratchet that drives (a ratchet feed) adds a rocking lever carrying the driving pawl, with a second, fixed pawl to hold the wheel while the lever swings back.
- **how_it_works**: The teeth are lopsided: one side of each tooth is a gentle slope and the other is steep. Turned the free way, the pawl slides up each gentle slope and a spring snaps it down behind the tooth, which makes the click; turned the other way, the pawl tip meets a steep face and jams, so the wheel stops (Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)'). The steep face is angled so that the load pulls the pawl deeper into the tooth rather than pushing it out (Machinery's Handbook), and something must always press the pawl in: a spring, the pawl's weight, or a springy pawl (Durley). A reversible ratchet uses teeth that can be caught from either side and a pawl you can flip, turn half a turn, or swap for a second pawl facing the other way, which is what the little lever on a socket wrench does (Machinery's Handbook; Wikipedia 'Socket wrench').

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - stop it moving back (one-way): the main job, e.g. holding a winch drum or hoist so the load can't run back
  - click into set positions: the wheel can only stop at a tooth, so it moves and holds in steps of one tooth
  - change the kind of motion: a pawl on a rocking lever turns back-and-forth movement into one-way, step-by-step rotation (ratchet feed, ratchet wrench)
  - tighten and hold tension: wind a reel, strap or cable tighter and it stays tight
  - lock, and release by hand: lifting the pawl by hand frees the wheel to run back
- **motion**: rotate: unlimited turning in the free direction; in the locked direction it can only creep back up to one tooth spacing before it catches. A reversible ratchet lets you choose which direction is free.
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the wheel can only turn about its own axle, and the pawl limits that turning to one direction (the pawl's own small swing is an internal motion, not a way for the parts to move apart)
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the defining behaviour; the wheel turns one way with a click and jams against the pawl the other way
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): it holds only at tooth positions, so it moves in steps; a pawl on a rocking lever can step a wheel round a set number of teeth each stroke (Machinery's Handbook, ratchet feed)
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): lifting the pawl lets the wheel run back, as on a winch or reel release
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): winding a drum or reel and letting the pawl hold each gain
  - motion conversion (e.g. rotary to linear): a rocking lever with a pawl gives one-way, intermittent rotation
  - reversible: in a reversible ratchet a selector (flip-over pawl, turned plunger pawl, or a switch that lifts one of two pawls) swaps the free and locked directions
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the pawl tip butts against the steep tooth face
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): a spring, gravity or a springy pawl keeps the pawl in the teeth; the mechanism must be 'force-closed' in this way (Durley)
- **input_output**: Used as a stop: rotation one way passes freely, rotation the other way is blocked; it is not back-drivable in the locked direction (the only way back is to lift the pawl). Used as a drive (ratchet feed or ratchet wrench): back-and-forth swinging of a lever -> one-way, stepped rotation of the wheel; each stroke advances a whole number of teeth, so the smallest step is 360 degrees divided by the number of teeth, and two pawls of different length offset by half a tooth halve that step (Machinery's Handbook). Shafts: the lever or frame and the wheel share one axle (in line). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **power**: passive (no power needed). If a motor drives through a ratchet and stops, the pawl keeps holding the load.
- **removability**: removable: the wheel is keyed, pinned or clamped to its shaft and the pawl sits on a pin or screw, so it can be taken apart and refitted (NotesandSketches describes the wheel keyed to the shaft)
- **reuse_cycles**: effectively unlimited: it is built to click thousands of times; the limit is wear of the tooth faces, pawl tip and pivot, not taking it apart
- **tools_needed**: Usually a screwdriver or hex key for the pawl pivot screw and frame, and sometimes circlip pliers for retaining clips; print-in-place printed ratchets need none, only a firm first twist to break the parts free
- **seal**: none: the teeth and pawl are open to dirt. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **load**: Light to heavy depending on size and material: steel ratchet wheels are sized with a formula used for crane ratchets (Machinery's Handbook), while a well-designed PLA print has reached about 30 N·m in a ratcheting wrench (Hackaday on Gladius's design). It is strong against turning in the locked direction, where the tooth is loaded in bending and shear and the pawl mostly in compression; the pawl pivot pin carries almost the whole reaction force (Firgelli, vendor explainer). The free direction carries no load. Weak points are the pawl pivot, tooth tips and, in prints, layer lines.
- **adjustable**: No fine adjustment: it can only hold at tooth positions. Finer steps come from more teeth, or from two or more pawls of different lengths offset by a fraction of a tooth (Machinery's Handbook); 'dual-pawl' socket wrenches click twice per tooth (Wikipedia 'Socket wrench').
- **play_and_precision**: noticeable: it can slip back by up to one tooth spacing before the pawl catches (this backward slip is called backlash; Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)'). Once caught it returns to the same tooth very repeatably; spring-blade indexing ratchets can position to about a tenth of a degree (RoyMech). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: not applicable: it is built into a mechanism (a winch, reel, tool head or gearbox) rather than joining two panels
- **materials**: Hardened steel wheels and pawls for hoists, winches and hand tools (pawl tips are hardened; Firgelli, vendor explainer); brass and steel in clocks; moulded acetal (POM, 'Delrin' (brand)) and nylon for plastic ratchets, often with moulded-in springs (DuPont design guide); 3D prints in PLA for demos and PETG, ASA or nylon for use; springs in spring steel, because plastic springs that stay loaded relax (DuPont).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: easy to print a working demo (a wheel, a pawl, an M3 screw and a small spring); moderate to make one that carries real torque, because the tooth angle, pawl position, spring and print orientation all have to be right

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): demos, fidget toys and light-to-medium duty tools, including print-in-place and reversible designs
  - CNC: steel ratchet wheels are milled with an angled cutter or hobbed with a special hob (Machinery's Handbook)
  - off-the-shelf part: ratchet wheels, pawls, pawl springs, and complete ratchet winches, hoists and wrenches *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: yes (with springy printed pawls) / partly (classic pivoting pawl): print-in-place ratchets exist, e.g. joeDAcoolGUY's bi-directional ratchet screwdriver and a print-in-place 1/4-inch bit ratchet on Printables, which use springy printed parts and about 0.3 mm gaps. The classic layout with a pivoting pawl prints as separate parts and needs an added pivot screw and a metal spring (e.g. the 'Ratchet and Pawl Demo' needs an M3 screw and an extension spring).
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Get the tooth and pawl geometry right first: make the locking face of each tooth steep (nearly pointing at the wheel centre) and place the pawl pivot so that a line drawn at right angles to the locking face passes between the wheel centre and the pawl pivot; then the load pulls the pawl in instead of throwing it out (Machinery's Handbook; for a pawl that pulls rather than pushes, the line must pass outside the pivot). A vendor explainer suggests a locking face of about 80-90 degrees to the tangent, with 85 degrees as a safe default (Firgelli). 2) Print the wheel and pawl lying flat (axle vertical), so the teeth and the pawl are loaded along the layers, not across them; prints are stronger in one direction, like wood (Prusa), and springy arms should not be built standing up in Z (Hubs). 3) For strength, use many perimeters or solid parts: a PLA ratcheting wrench reached about 30 N·m by slicing parts to be almost entirely perimeters (Hackaday on Gladius's design); Owenberry's 1/4-inch drive ratchet was printed in PETG at 100% infill. 4) Tooth size matters more than tooth count: in joeDAcoolGUY's tests the real failure mode was tooth flex and too little engagement length, and a 6 mm thick pawl on a 22 mm ratchet ring worked. 5) Clearances: start at 0.3 mm for parts that must move (Prusa); joeDAcoolGUY used 0.3 mm for free-moving parts and 0.15 mm where he wanted controlled friction. Watch for elephant's foot on small parts, which can jam the mechanism (Martin Rigatoni's reversible screwdriver notes, via the Printables API). 6) Springs: use a small metal spring (an extension spring, or half a pen spring as in Owenberry's design) to hold the pawl in; printed springs that stay loaded relax over time (DuPont), and one maker withdrew a printed ratchet screwdriver because it 'suffers from creep so doesn't last' (Printables API, model 82837). Springy printed pawls are fine for occasional light use, in PETG or nylon rather than PLA. 7) Use a metal screw or pin as the pawl pivot and don't clamp it tight; the 'Ratchet and Pawl Demo' says to leave the M3 screw slightly loose so the pawl can swing. 8) Reversible ratchets: the selector has to be guided or pushed from both sides, otherwise friction jams it once everything is enclosed (joeDAcoolGUY). 9) Print-in-place parts may need a firm first twist to break them free, and a little lubricant (silicone spray or light oil) makes the click smoother. 10) Material: PLA starts to soften at about 60 °C, PETG at about 80 °C and ASA at about 110 °C (CNC Kitchen), so don't leave a PLA ratchet in a hot car.
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search terms: 'ratchet wheel' or 'ratchet gear', 'pawl', 'ratchet and pawl set', 'pawl spring' (usually a small torsion or extension spring), 'shoulder screw' or 'dowel pin' for the pivot, 'retaining ring' or 'E-clip'. Complete products that contain one: 'ratchet wrench' or 'socket wrench' (reversible, with 'fine-tooth' and 'dual-pawl' versions), 'ratcheting screwdriver', 'hand winch' with ratchet, 'ratchet lever hoist' (come-along), fishing-reel anti-reverse, and pawl-type bicycle freewheels. Silent, pawl-free alternatives are sold as 'one-way clutch', 'sprag clutch' or 'one-way needle bearing'.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Pawl position: the locking face should be placed so that a line at right angles to it passes between the wheel centre and the pawl pivot when the pawl pushes the wheel (or the wheel pushes the pawl); when the pawl pulls, that line should fall outside the pawl pivot. Then the pawl stays engaged under load (Machinery's Handbook, 27th ed., 'Shape of Ratchet Wheel Teeth', p. 2089).
  - Tooth pitch for a steel ratchet holding a hanging load: P = sqrt(F·M / (L·S·N)), where P = circular pitch at the tips, M = turning moment, L = face width (wheel thickness), N = number of teeth, S = safe stress (steel: 2500 psi / 17 MPa with shock, 4000 psi / 28 MPa without), F = 50 for 12 teeth or fewer, 35 for 12-20 teeth, 20 for over 20 teeth; used for crane ratchets (Machinery's Handbook p. 2089; also on Engineers Edge citing the 29th edition).
  - Finer steps without weaker teeth: use several pawls whose lengths differ by a fraction of the tooth pitch; two pawls differing by half a pitch halve the step (Machinery's Handbook p. 2089). Rob Ives also notes two pawls reduce the stop-start motion.
  - Resolution and backlash: the wheel can only hold every 360°/N (N = number of teeth), so it may slip back up to one tooth before catching (Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)'); dual-pawl socket wrenches click twice per tooth (Wikipedia 'Socket wrench').
  - The pawl must always be pressed into the teeth ('force-closed'): by a spring, by its own weight, or by making the pawl itself a spring; and its shape must be such that the tooth pressure does not throw it out of gear (Durley, Kinematics of Machines, ch. IX, p. 230).
  - At light loads a driven ratchet wheel can jump ahead past the intended tooth; add a brake or detent, or watch the load (RoyMech 'Ratchets').
  - Plastic ratchets with moulded-in springs work well 'providing the springs are not held in the loaded position for a long period of time'; springs under constant load or deflection should be spring steel (DuPont 'General Design Principles for Engineering Polymers', pp. 36 and 47).
  - Moving printed parts need at least about 0.3 mm clearance as a starting point (Prusa knowledge base).
  - For springy (flexure) pawls: don't build them standing up in Z, and add a fillet at the root of at least half the arm thickness (Hubs snap-fit guide).
  - Vendor guidance (not a standard): make the locking face 80-90 degrees to the tangent, 85 degrees by default, and keep the pawl pivot pin snug, because pivot wear shifts the engagement angle (Firgelli 'Pawl' explainer).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A socket wrench (ratchet spanner): the small lever on the head switches which way it clicks (reversible ratchet)
  - A ratcheting screwdriver or a ratcheting bit driver
  - A hand winch, boat trailer winch or come-along (ratchet lever hoist) that holds the load while you rest
  - A bicycle freewheel (most use spring-loaded pawls in a toothed ring) and the anti-reverse click in fishing reels and yacht winches
- **when_to_use**: Use a ratchet and pawl when something should turn one way but never slip back: winding a winch, reel or spool, holding a lifted load while you reposition your hand, or tightening something step by step. It is also the simplest way to turn a back-and-forth lever into one-way stepped rotation (a ratchet wrench, a ratchet feed or a counter), and it gives an audible, felt click at each tooth. Choose the reversible version when you need to tighten and loosen with the same tool, as on socket wrenches and screwdrivers. It is cheap, easy to understand, easy to print, and you can release it on purpose by lifting the pawl.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it when you need to hold at any angle with no slip-back (it only holds at tooth positions): use a friction brake, a sprag clutch or a roller clutch (one-way bearing), which lock almost instantly with no backlash, or a worm drive. Avoid it where the clicking noise, wear or bounce at high speed are a problem (a vendor explainer puts the practical limit for ordinary spring-loaded pawls at a few hundred rpm, Firgelli): use a sprag or roller clutch. For a linear strip use a linear ratchet, and for a one-piece printed part with no pins or springs use a compliant (flexure-pawl) ratchet. Don't rely on a home-made ratchet to hold people or heavy loads overhead; use rated hardware.
- **environment_limits**: Printed plastics soften with heat: PLA starts to give at about 60 °C, PETG about 80 °C, ASA about 110 °C (CNC Kitchen), so a PLA ratchet in a hot car may deform. Plastic springs and springy pawls lose force if they stay loaded (stress relaxation, DuPont), and PLA creeps most under constant load (thrinter creep test), so a printed pawl held flexed may stop snapping in fully. Vibration or very light loads can make a driven wheel jump ahead (RoyMech). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: The pawl and teeth form a pinch point for fingers. Pawl springs can fly out when a mechanism is opened. A worn or badly shaped ratchet can skip under load, so home-made or printed ratchets must never be used for lifting people or holding loads overhead. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: 1) The pawl skips or is pushed out under load, because the locking face or pivot position is wrong (the load pushes the pawl out instead of pulling it in) or the pivot is worn: follow the Machinery's Handbook rule and keep the pivot snug. 2) The pawl doesn't drop in (it floats over the teeth) because the spring is too weak or has relaxed, the pawl is sticky, or dirt and grease are in the way: use a metal spring and keep it clean. 3) Tooth tips round over, chip or snap, or, in prints, shear off along layer lines: make teeth larger rather than more numerous (joeDAcoolGUY), print flat with many perimeters. 4) The wheel overshoots at light load (RoyMech): add a detent or light brake. 5) Too much slip-back (backlash) for the job: use finer teeth or several offset pawls, or a sprag or roller clutch. 7) Print-in-place parts are fused: increase clearance (about 0.3 mm) and check elephant's foot. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet): the same idea on a straight toothed strip, so it slides one way instead of turning
  - Face ratchet (star ratchet): the teeth are on the faces of two spring-pressed discs, so every tooth engages at once instead of one pawl
  - Compliant ratchet (flexure pawl): the pawl is a springy printed arm, so there are no pins or separate springs
  - Sprag clutch and roller clutch (one-way bearing): one-way locking by wedging, silent and with almost no backlash, but no audible click and no step positions
  - Wrap-spring clutch: a coiled spring grips a shaft one way; smooth, silent, one-way
  - Ratchet-tooth torque limiter: similar sloped spring-loaded teeth, but shaped so they slip when the torque gets too high
  - Ball detent / indexing detent: clicks into positions and holds both ways, but can be pushed through with enough force
  - Escapement: a ratchet-like wheel released one tooth at a time to control speed (clocks)
  - Worm drive: holds a load at any position (self-locking), not just at teeth, but is slow and inefficient
  - Geneva drive: another way to get step-by-step rotation, with smooth locked dwell between steps

## 8 Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet)

*one-way & ratchet.* A toothed strip and a catch that let it slide one way only, as in a cable tie, a quick-release bar clamp or a ratchet strap

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A strip or bar with saw-like teeth and a springy catch that lets it slide through one way with a zipping or clicking sound but stops it sliding back, like a cable tie
- **term**: Linear ratchet (ratchet rack and pawl, toothed rack with pawl)
- **aliases**: 
  - rack and pawl / ratchet rack and pawl
  - ratchet bar / ratchet rack
  - linear ratchet mechanism
  - cable tie ratchet / zip tie / tie wrap / Ty-Rap (brand, the original cable tie)
  - ladder strap and ratchet buckle (snowboard bindings, ski boots)
  - ratchet buckle
  - running ratchet acting on a rack (Durley)
  - ratchet jack / lifting jack pawls (Machinery's Handbook, Fig. i) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **family**: one-way & ratchet
- **other_families**: tensioning, reels & take-up, cord, strap, webbing & textile hardware, sliding, telescoping & linear motion
- **one_liner**: A toothed strip or bar and a catch (pawl) that let it slide one way only, locking at each tooth, as in a cable tie, a snowboard-binding ladder strap or a ratchet jack.
- **complexity**: From a single piece to a few parts. A cable tie is one moulded piece: a flexible toothed tape and a head with a moulded-in pawl (Wikipedia 'Cable tie'). A rigid linear ratchet is a few parts: a toothed bar (rack), a pawl, a pivot and a spring, inside a guide that stops the bar wandering sideways. A ratchet buckle adds a lever that pushes the strap along and a release catch; a ratchet jack uses two pawls, one on the operating lever to lift and one fixed to hold (Machinery's Handbook).
- **how_it_works**: The strip's teeth are lopsided, with a gentle slope on one side and a steep face on the other. Pushed or pulled the free way, the springy pawl rides up each slope and snaps behind the next tooth; pulled the other way, the pawl catches a steep face and locks (Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)'). In a cable tie the pawl sits inside the head, so once the tip has passed the pawl the tape can't be pulled back 'unless a large amount of force is applied' (Wikipedia 'Cable tie'). To tighten repeatedly, a second pawl on a lever can push the strip on one tooth per stroke while the fixed pawl holds it, as in a lifting jack or a snowboard ratchet buckle (Machinery's Handbook; Burton patent US7086122).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - stop it moving back (one-way): the strip can go in but not come back out
  - tighten and hold tension: pull the tail of a cable tie or pump a buckle lever and it stays as tight as you left it
  - join and hold: a cable tie bundles or fixes cables and parts together
  - click into set positions: it holds at one-tooth steps along its length
  - lock, and release by hand: releasable ties, ski and snowboard buckles and ratchet clamps have a lever or tab that lifts the pawl
- **motion**: slide: straight-line travel one way over the length of the toothed strip, in steps of one tooth; curved versions (e.g. the swinging toothed arm of handcuffs) follow an arc
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the strip can only slide along its own length, and the pawl limits that sliding to one direction
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the defining behaviour
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): each tooth gained is kept
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): it can only hold at tooth positions
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): releasable ties, buckles and ratchet clamps lift the pawl with a tab or lever
  - quick-release: ski-boot and snowboard ratchet buckles let go of the ladder strap with one release lever (Burton patent US7086122)
  - none for standard cable ties once tightened: they are meant to be cut off, not released
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the pawl hooks behind a tooth
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): the pawl is springy or spring-loaded so it always drops into the next tooth
- **input_output**: Mostly not applicable: as a fastener it just holds. When driven (ratchet jack, ratchet buckle, ratchet feed), back-and-forth lever strokes -> step-by-step one-way linear motion, one or more teeth per stroke, with a second pawl holding during the return stroke (Machinery's Handbook, Fig. i; Burton patent US7086122). It cannot be back-driven: pulling the strip backwards only jams the pawl harder, until the pawl is lifted.
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: Standard cable tie: permanent in practice (cut it off; it can be opened by pushing a small flat object between the pawl and the teeth, but that damages it; Wikipedia 'Cable tie'). Releasable cable ties, ratchet clamps and ski or snowboard buckles: tool-free quick release. Rigid racks and pawls: removable.
- **reuse_cycles**: Standard cable tie: once. Buckles, jacks and rigid ratchet bars: many times, limited by tooth wear. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **tools_needed**: None to fit a cable tie; side cutters or flush cutters to remove it. A cable tie tensioning tool (tie gun) pulls to a set tension and cuts the tail off flush so no sharp edge is left (Wikipedia 'Cable tie'). Rigid versions need a screwdriver or hex key for the pawl pivot.
- **seal**: none
- **load**: Light to medium for plastic versions and heavy for steel jacks. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **adjustable**: yes, in steps of one tooth: you can always tighten further; loosening needs the pawl to be released (or a new tie)
- **play_and_precision**: noticeable: it can slide back up to one tooth spacing before locking (Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)'). Two pawls offset by half a tooth halve the step (Machinery's Handbook). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: both sides: a cable tie or strap has to pass right round the object, though you tighten and cut it from one side; a rigid rack-and-pawl is built into a mechanism
- **materials**: Cable ties: usually nylon 6,6; outdoor ties are UV-stabilised black nylon with carbon black (at least 2%, Wikipedia; available only in black, Nelco); heat-stabilised nylon, polypropylene, Tefzel and PEEK for heat or chemicals; blue ties with a metal additive for metal detectors; stainless steel ties for heat and corrosion (Wikipedia 'Cable tie'; Nelco). Jacks and industrial racks: steel. 3D prints: PLA+ works for light-duty printed cable ties, PETG or nylon for heavier use (Printables 'Zip/Cable Tie'). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: a printed zip tie or a rack and pawl is easy to make, but printed straps are weaker and creep more than moulded nylon ties, so for real use it is easier to buy cable ties

### Making it

- **processes**: off-the-shelf part: cable ties, releasable ties, cable tie mounts, ratchet buckles with ladder straps, 3D print (FDM): printable zip ties, ratchet clamps and print-in-place ratcheting capos *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: yes: a printable cable tie comes off the bed as one working piece (Printables 'Zip/Cable Tie', 7.2 inches long, about 10 minutes to print), and a ratcheting guitar capo prints in place without supports (Mikolas Zuza's remix of LoboCNC's design, via the Printables API). Designs with a pivoting pawl and a metal spring are 'partly'.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Print strips and pawls lying flat, so the strap is made of long continuous lines along its length and the springy pawl bends within the layers, not across them; prints are stronger in one direction (Prusa) and springy arms should not be built standing up in Z (Hubs). 3) Make the part that carries the pawl stiff: in the ratcheting capo remix, the original ratchet arm bent under load so the ratchet did not engage at all in the first half of its travel, which the remix fixed (Printables API, model 63610). 4) Material: PLA+ is fine for a light-duty printed cable tie; use PETG or a more flexible material such as nylon for heavier use (Printables 'Zip/Cable Tie'). A tie that holds tension all the time will creep, and PLA creeps the most (thrinter creep test), so re-tighten or use PETG or nylon. 7) Add a pull-tab to the pawl if you want to release and reuse it. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: The common truck 'ratchet strap' is a different device: a lever ratchet that winds webbing onto a spool, covered by the rotary ratchet entry. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - A linear ratchet that lifts step by step needs two pawls: one on the operating lever does the lifting, and a second, fixed pawl holds the load while the lever is returned (Machinery's Handbook, 27th ed., p. 2089, Fig. i, lifting jack).
  - Several pawls offset by a fraction of the tooth pitch give finer steps from strong, coarse teeth; two pawls differing by half a pitch halve the step (Machinery's Handbook p. 2089).
  - The strip can slip back by up to one tooth before it locks (Wikipedia 'Ratchet (device)').
  - For outdoor use choose UV-stabilised black nylon with at least 2% carbon black (Wikipedia 'Cable tie').
  - Nylon 6/6 cable ties: -40 °F to 185 °F (-40 °C to 85 °C) continuous; heat-stabilised nylon 6/6 to 220 °F (about 104 °C); PEEK -76 °F to 500 °F (Nelco material specifications).
  - Cut the tail flush with the head (a tensioning tool does this) so no sharp edge is left (Wikipedia 'Cable tie').
  - Many one-handed bar clamps don't use teeth at all: their drive lever and brake are plates with inclined holes that grip the top and bottom edges of a smooth bar and can occasionally slip; US patent 6585243 adds teeth for a positive (ratchet) grip (Google Patents US6585243B1).
  - Printed moving parts need about 0.3 mm clearance as a starting point (Prusa knowledge base); don't build springy arms standing up in Z (Hubs).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A cable tie (zip tie) holding cables together
  - The toothed ladder strap and ratchet buckle on snowboard bindings and some ski boots
  - Handcuffs, whose swinging toothed arm ratchets tighter and can't be opened back (a curved linear ratchet) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **when_to_use**: Use a linear ratchet when something must be pulled or pushed tighter in one direction and must not slip back: bundling cables, strapping a part to a frame, closing a clamp or a strap round an object of unknown size, or lifting something in steps with a lever (a ratchet jack). Cable ties are the quickest, cheapest way to hold things together in prototypes and robots. Toothed-bar ratchets also appear in linear clamps and quick-release vices (DT Online), although many quick-grip style bar clamps grip a smooth bar with tilting plates instead (see binding-plate lock). Choose releasable ties, ratchet buckles or clamps with a release tab when you need to undo and redo it.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it when you need smooth, stepless adjustment (use a binding-plate lock, a cam buckle or a screw clamp), when you need to loosen as often as you tighten (use a buckle, hook-and-loop or a screw), when the load must be released gently under tension (use a screw or a winch with a brake), or for permanent structural joints (use screws or bolts). Don't use cable ties to hold heavy loads overhead, anything hot above their rating, or anything that moves and chafes the tie. For a rotating shaft use a rotary ratchet and pawl or a one-way clutch.
- **environment_limits**: Standard nylon 6/6 ties are rated about -40 °C to 85 °C continuous, heat-stabilised nylon to about 104 °C (Nelco). Sunlight degrades natural (white) nylon ties; use UV-stabilised black ones outdoors (Wikipedia; Nelco). Stainless steel ties are for high heat and corrosive places (Wikipedia). Printed PLA ties soften from about 60 °C (CNC Kitchen) and creep under constant tension (thrinter creep test), so they loosen over time. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Cut tails leave sharp edges that can cut hands and cables; cut flush or use a tensioning tool (Wikipedia 'Cable tie'). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: 2) The tie snaps or the head cracks, often from overloading, sunlight (UV) or heat: use UV-black or heat-stabilised ties, or stainless ties. 3) Printed ties and clamps loosen over time from creep, especially in PLA: print in PETG or nylon and re-tighten. 4) The pawl doesn't engage because the part holding it bends (as in the original ratcheting capo, via the Printables API): stiffen that part. 5) Too coarse steps for a snug fit: use finer teeth or two offset pawls (Machinery's Handbook). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Ratchet and pawl (rotary): the same mechanism on a toothed wheel, for winches, reels and wrenches
  - Ratchet tie-down strap (ratchet buckle for webbing): despite the name it winds the strap onto a spool with a rotary ratchet, not a toothed strip
  - Binding-plate lock (tilting friction plate): grips a smooth bar anywhere, with no teeth, as in most quick-grip bar clamps and many caulking guns; stepless but can slip
  - Compliant ratchet (flexure pawl): one-piece printed ratchets whose pawl is a springy arm, which is how a cable tie head works
  - Cam buckle and cam cleat: grip a strap or rope anywhere by a toothed or smooth cam, stepless
  - Ladder-lock buckle and guy-line slider: hold straps and cords by friction, easy to adjust both ways
  - Hook-and-loop strap (Velcro (brand)): reusable, adjustable anywhere, much weaker
  - Worm-drive hose clamp: tightens a band smoothly with a screw and can be loosened
  - Rope grab (ascender): a toothed cam that slides up a rope one way and grips the other
