## 13 Compliant ratchet and overrunning clutch (flexure pawl)

*one-way & ratchet.* One-piece printed ratchets and one-way clutches whose pawls are springy arms, so there are no pins or separate springs

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A one-piece plastic click-wheel whose catches are springy arms: it turns one way with a click and locks the other way, with no pins or separate springs
- **term**: Compliant ratchet (flexure-pawl ratchet); when used as a freewheel, a compliant overrunning clutch
- **aliases**: 
  - flexure pawl
  - compliant pawl
  - flex pawl
  - spring pawl / leaf-spring pawl / flat-spring pawl
  - spring-blade ratchet (RoyMech's name for metal versions whose pawls are flat springs)
  - ratchet with molded-in springs (DuPont's description of injection-moulded plastic versions)
  - compliant over-running ratchet and pawl clutch (BYU Compliant Mechanisms Research)
  - compliant overrunning clutch with centrifugal throw-out (US patent 6,148,979)
  - one-piece ratchet / monolithic ratchet
  - print-in-place ratchet
  - compliant freewheel
- **family**: one-way & ratchet
- **other_families**: compliant mechanisms & flexures, print-in-place & 3D-printing joints, indexing & detents
- **one_liner**: A ratchet or one-way clutch in which the catch (pawl) and its spring are the same springy arm, moulded or printed in one piece with the hub, so the wheel clicks round one way and locks the other with no pins, springs or assembly.
- **complexity**: A few parts, often just one: (1) a toothed wheel or toothed ring (ratchet wheel); (2) a hub or frame carrying one or more thin springy arms whose tips are the catches (flexure pawls). The hub, arms and catches are a single piece. Print-in-place versions also trap the wheel inside the frame, so the whole thing is one printed part. The BYU compliant overrunning clutch is two parts: a one-piece pawl hub sitting inside a ratchet ring (US 6,148,979).
- **how_it_works**: Each catch sits on the end of a thin arm that bends like a diving board (a cantilever flexure), so the arm is both the hinge and the spring. Turned the free way, the gently sloped backs of the teeth push the catch aside, bending the arm, and it springs back into the next gap with a click. Turned the other way, the steep face of a tooth pushes straight against the catch's tip; the tooth face is shaped so this push drives the catch deeper into the tooth rather than out of it, so the wheel locks. In BYU's patented clutch, stiff support members carry the locking load so the slender spring arms only have to bias the pawls, and at high freewheeling speed the pawls swing outward by centrifugal force (centrifugal throw-out) so they stop rubbing on the teeth (US 6,148,979).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - stop it moving back (one-way): it turns or slides freely one way and locks the other way
  - click into set positions: every tooth is a click stop
  - tighten and hold tension: winders, knobs and cable ties keep whatever they have pulled in
- **motion**: rotate: unlimited turns in the free direction, locked in the other (linear versions such as a cable tie slide instead)
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the wheel only turns about its axle (or the strip only slides along its length), and under load only in one direction
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the springy catches ride over the sloped backs of the teeth one way and jam against the steep tooth faces the other way
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): it stops and clicks at every tooth, so it holds in steps of one tooth
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): in winders and cable ties it keeps the tension you wind or pull in
  - BYU variant only: centrifugal throw-out, meaning the pawls swing clear of the teeth at speed, so the freewheeling clutch runs with less rubbing, noise and wear (US 6,148,979)
- **holding_principle**: shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the tip of the catch hooks against a tooth face, spring force (a springy part presses it in place): the bent arm keeps the catch pressed into the teeth
- **input_output**: Rotation in the driving direction -> the same rotation at 1:1 (it is a clutch, not a gearbox); in the other direction the wheel freewheels. Shafts in line. Efficient when driving because nothing slides once a catch is engaged; there is a small drag from the arms rubbing over the teeth when freewheeling. Back-drivable: no in the locking direction; free in the other direction. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: removable: two-part designs (pawl hub plus ratchet ring) slide apart; print-in-place versions are captive, so in practice they are permanent unless you cut them
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: there are no fasteners to wear out; the life limit is fatigue of the springy arms, not taking it apart
- **tools_needed**: none: it prints or moulds in one piece; two-part versions push together by hand
- **seal**: none: it is an open mechanism and nothing makes it seal
- **load**: Light to medium. Weak if the catch is loaded sideways or the thin arm has to carry the lock. Plastic versions suit hand-powered and small-motor loads, not lifting people or heavy overhead loads. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **adjustable**: no: the click force is set by the arm's length and thickness; to change it you reprint or remould with a different arm
- **play_and_precision**: It returns to tooth positions repeatably. It tolerates a little wobble because the arms flex, but the catch tips must stay lined up with the teeth. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the hub or wheel slides onto the end of a shaft; print-in-place demos need no access at all
- **materials**: Moulded versions: polypropylene (named in the BYU patent because it is cheap and easy to mould); acetal (Delrin) gears with molded-in springs acting on a nylon ratchet wheel (DuPont design guide, Fig. 8.01); nylon (cable ties). Metal versions: heat-treated flat spring steel blades (RoyMech). 3D printing: PETG or nylon for the springy arms; PLA 'makes for a very poor spring material' (Hackaday). It is a mechanism, not a joining method, so it does not 'join' materials.
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: it prints in one piece, but getting the arm stiffness, tip clearance and tooth shape right usually takes a few test prints

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): the usual maker method; prints in one piece
  - sheet metal: spring-steel blade pawls in metal ratchets (RoyMech, spring blade ratchet)
  - off-the-shelf part: a cable tie is a ready-made linear ratchet with a moulded flexing catch *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: yes: the wheel, the springy catches and the frame can print already assembled and working, provided there is clearance between the moving parts (Prusa suggests at least 0.3 mm as a starting point)
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Orientation: print it lying flat so the arms bend sideways in the plane of the bed. Each arm is then built from continuous extrusion lines running along its length, and bending does not try to peel layers apart (Hubs' living-hinge rule: orient so the hinge's width, not its length, is built up layer by layer). Arm size: make each arm at least two perimeters wide (about 0.9 mm with a 0.4 mm nozzle; one perimeter, about 0.45 mm, is the absolute minimum, Prusa). For a given bend, stress rises with thickness and falls with length (standard cantilever formula, quoted in the print-bench design brief), so prefer longer, thinner arms to short, thick ones. Tapering the arm toward its tip (a constant-strength beam) gives the same stiffness at about two-thirds of the stress (DuPont, p. 36). Add a generous fillet where the arm joins the hub, because sharp corners start fatigue cracks (DuPont, p. 36). Clearance: leave at least 0.3 mm between wheel and frame and around the catch tips (Prusa), and check the first layer, since a squashed first layer (elephant's foot) is a common reason print-in-place parts fuse. Material: PETG or nylon for the arms; avoid PLA for anything that must click many times (Hackaday). Rest state: design so the catches sit in the tooth gaps with little or no bend when stopped; plastic springs held bent for long periods creep and lose force (DuPont, pp. 36-37 and 47). Tooth shape: make the locking face of each tooth point straight at the centre (radial) or slightly undercut, so the load pulls the catch in, and make the back of the tooth a shallow slope so the catch rides over it (one hobby brief used a radial locking flank and about a 60° drive flank). Use two or more catches spread round the wheel to share and balance the load. Typical print failures: an arm snapping at its root (add a fillet, taper it, use PETG or nylon, reduce how far it bends), arms taking a set so the clicks get weak (reduce preload), catches skipping under load (steeper locking face, stiffer arms, more catches) and fused parts (more clearance).
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Few ready-made compliant ratchets are sold as parts. Useful search terms: 'cable tie' and 'releasable cable tie' (linear version with a moulded flexing pawl); 'flat spring', 'leaf spring' or 'spring steel shim' (to make a metal spring-blade pawl); 'ratchet wheel' (plastic or steel, to pair with a printed flexure pawl); 'one-way bearing' or 'one-way needle roller clutch' (a silent ready-made alternative). BYU's compliant overrunning clutch is shared as a free printable model (Thingiverse thing:3007265).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Plastic springs suit intermittent action, not constant load: 'Springs under constant load or deflection should be designed in spring steel' because of creep and stress relaxation (DuPont, General Design Principles, Module I, p. 36).
  - Ratchets with molded-in acetal springs 'function properly, providing the springs are not held in the loaded position for a long period of time' (DuPont, Module I, p. 47).
  - A tapered (constant-strength) spring arm reaches the same stiffness at two-thirds of the stress of a straight arm and with a quarter less material (DuPont, Module I, p. 36, Fig. 6.01).
  - Avoid sharp corners at the arm root; use generous fillets (DuPont, Module I, p. 36).
  - Start with at least 0.3 mm clearance between printed moving parts; the minimum printable wall is one extrusion width, about 0.45 mm for a 0.4 mm nozzle (Prusa Knowledge Base, Modeling with 3D printing in mind).
  - FDM living hinges: 0.4-0.6 mm thick with at least two layers, oriented so the hinge width is built up layer by layer; a nylon FDM example lasted only about 25 full folds, so a printed ratchet arm should bend far less than a folding hinge (Hubs, How to design living hinges for 3D printing).
  - Any ratchet can slip back by up to one tooth pitch before it catches (Wikipedia, Ratchet (device)).
  - Metal spring-blade ratchets, with proper heat treatment, give long life under light loading and can index to about one-tenth of a degree (RoyMech, Ratchets). *(unverified parts omitted)*

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A cable tie: the small tongue inside the head is a springy catch moulded in one piece with the head, clicking over the teeth on the strap (Wikipedia, Cable tie; Wikimedia Commons cutaway photo)
  - Timers and clockwork that wind springs and drive timing cams with flat-spring pawls (RoyMech, spring blade ratchet)
  - Plastic gear-and-ratchet parts in small machines, with the pawl springs moulded into the gear (DuPont design example, Fig. 8.01)
- **when_to_use**: When you want a one-way or click-stop action in a printed or moulded part without buying springs and pins: winders and take-up spools, anti-reverse on a hand crank, one-way knobs, simple freewheels in toys and light hand tools, and teaching models. It is a good choice when the part should be cheap, one piece, and assemble in seconds (or not at all), when loads are light, and when the catch spends most of its time resting in a tooth gap rather than held bent.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it for heavy or safety-critical holding (hoists, anything above people): use a steel ratchet and pawl with a metal spring, or a worm drive. Avoid it where a catch must stay bent for long periods, because plastic springs creep and lose force (DuPont): use a metal spring. Avoid it when clicking or the one-tooth backlash matters: use a roller clutch or sprag clutch (silent, no tooth steps) or a wrap-spring clutch. Avoid PLA arms for high cycle counts: use PETG, nylon or a metal spring blade. Avoid fast freewheeling unless the design allows for it (the BYU centrifugal throw-out is one answer).
- **environment_limits**: All plastic springs creep and relax if held bent, and temperature and chemicals change their spring properties (DuPont, Module I, p. 36); PLA is a very poor spring material (Hackaday, 2026). Nylon parts used outdoors need UV stabilising; outdoor cable ties use nylon with at least 2% carbon black (Wikipedia, Cable tie). Vibration is usually fine because a catch sits in a tooth gap, but a weak arm can let the catch bounce out. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Low hazard in small demos. Do not use a printed flexure ratchet as the only thing holding a raised load or a tension that could whip back, because the arms can fail suddenly from fatigue. Small printed parts are a choking hazard for young children.
- **common_failures**: Arm cracks at its root after many clicks (fatigue, the main weakness of all flexing mechanisms because stress concentrates where they bend; Wikipedia, Compliant mechanism): add a fillet, taper the arm, use PETG or nylon, and reduce how far it bends. Arm takes a permanent bend so clicks become weak or it skips (creep): don't leave catches preloaded; use a tougher material or a metal spring. Catches skip or pop out under load: make the locking tooth face radial or undercut so the load pulls the catch in, add more catches, stiffen the arms. Print-in-place parts fused together: increase clearance to 0.3 mm or more and fix the first layer. Teeth or catch tips wear round: larger teeth or a harder material. Loud clicking or drag when freewheeling: lighter arms, fewer catches, or a centrifugal throw-out design (BYU).
- **related**: 
  - Ratchet and pawl: the classic version with a pinned pawl and a separate spring; stronger and serviceable, but more parts
  - Face ratchet: teeth on the faces of two discs pressed together by a spring; often used as a printed ratcheting joint
  - Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet): the straight-line version; a cable tie is a linear flexure-pawl ratchet
  - Roller clutch (one-way bearing) and sprag clutch: silent one-way clutches with no tooth steps, but need hardened parts
  - Wrap-spring clutch: a coil spring grips a shaft one way; smooth and silent
  - Torque-limiting gear with flexing spring arms (compliant slip hub): same one-piece springy-arm idea, but made to slip when overloaded instead of locking
  - Detent: a springy catch that holds set positions but can be pushed past in both directions

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [File:ZipTieCrossSection.JPG (Wikimedia Commons)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:ZipTieCrossSection.JPG): Cutaway photo of a cable tie head showing the moulded springy catch (flexure pawl) against the strap's teeth *(licence: Public domain (released by the author, Mushrooshi))*
  - [Ratchet mechanism for anti-reverse 1 (Nguyen Duc Thang, thang010146)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rKYTr9NjgOA): CAD animation of a ratchet wheel that turns only one way because a flat spring acts as the pawl (description from Thang's catalogue, Part 2, p. 31) *(licence: link only)*
  - [US6148979A: Compliant overrunning clutch with centrifugal throw-out (Roach and Howell, BYU)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US6148979A/en): Patent drawings of the one-piece pawl hub with slender biasing arms and support members inside a ratchet ring *(licence: patent document; link only)* *(unverified parts omitted)*

## 14 Binding-plate lock (tilting friction plate)

*one-way & ratchet.* A plate with a hole slides along a bar, then tilts and bites when pushed the other way, as in caulking guns and quick-grip bar clamps

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A plate with a hole that slides along a rod or bar one way, but tips slightly and bites into it when pushed back, like the plates inside a caulking gun or a one-handed bar clamp
- **term**: Binding-plate lock (tilting friction plate; canted-plate linear clutch)
- **aliases**: 
  - tilting friction plate
  - canted plate / canting plate
  - jam plate
  - friction plate or lock plate (caulking guns)
  - drive plate or gripping plate (the plate the trigger tips to push the rod)
  - release plate or holding plate (the plate that stops the rod sliding back)
  - clutch plates (pipe clamps and bar clamps)
  - driving lever and braking lever (patent terms in one-handed bar clamps)
  - feeding plate and holding plate (patent terms in caulking guns)
  - binding washer
  - smooth-rod or friction-drive caulking gun mechanism
  - Quick-Grip (brand) one-handed bar clamp mechanism
  - sticky drawer effect (the same jamming, used on purpose)
- **family**: one-way & ratchet
- **other_families**: self-locking & anti-back-drive, clamping, magnetic & friction connections, sliding, telescoping & linear motion
- **one_liner**: A flat plate with a slightly oversized hole that slides freely along a rod while it sits square, but tips a few degrees and wedges the edges of its hole against the rod when pushed the other way, giving a stepless one-way lock that you release by pushing the plate square again.
- **complexity**: A few parts: a smooth rod or flat bar; one or more steel plates with a hole a little bigger than the rod; a small spring that keeps the plate tipped; a housing or edge the plate leans on; and a release lever or thumb plate that pushes it square. Drive versions (caulking gun, one-handed bar clamp) add a second plate that a trigger tips and pushes forward.
- **how_it_works**: When the plate sits square to the rod, the hole is a loose fit and the rod slides through. A spring tips the plate slightly, so opposite edges of the hole touch opposite sides of the rod. If the rod is pushed the locking way, friction at those two edges tries to tip the plate further, which squeezes the edges harder, so the grip grows with the load, just as a cocked drawer sticks harder the more you push it. Pushed the other way, the rod drags the plate back toward square and it slides. Whether it locks depends on friction against proportions: a tipped part binds when the friction coefficient is at least its engagement length divided by its width (Kalsi Seals Handbook, ch. D21), so a thin plate on a thick rod binds easily. In caulking guns and one-handed clamps the trigger tips a second plate so it grabs the rod and pushes it forward a little on each squeeze, while the holding plate stops it sliding back (US 2,530,359; US 4,081,112; US 4,926,722).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - stop it moving back (one-way): the rod or bar slides one way and is held against pushing back
  - lock, and release by hand: press the release plate and it goes square and lets go
  - tighten and hold tension: clamps and caulking guns use it to push and keep pushing
  - change the kind of motion: in trigger tools, squeezing the handle becomes a step-by-step straight push along the rod
- **motion**: slide: straight along the rod or bar; free and stepless (no notches) one way, locked the other way until released
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the plate and rod move only along the rod's length (the rod could also turn in the hole, but that is not used); under load the lock removes that freedom in one direction
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the tipped plate slides one way and bites the other
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): the harder the load pushes, the harder the plate's edges bite
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): pressing the release plate square frees the rod, as with a caulking gun's thumb plate or a bar clamp's release lever
  - quick-release: one press and the rod or jaw slides freely for fast repositioning
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds): it pushes clamp jaws or a caulk plunger and keeps the pressure on
  - stepless holding: unlike a toothed ratchet it can hold at any point along the rod
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): the edges of the hole grip the rod by friction, wedged tighter by the load
  - spring force (a springy part presses it in place): a small spring keeps the plate tipped so it is ready to bite
- **input_output**: Holding version: not a motion converter; it simply stops the rod moving back (back-drivable: no, unless the rod is oily, the plate edges are worn, or the plate is held square). Drive version (caulking gun, one-handed bar clamp): repeated trigger squeezes (back-and-forth swing) -> a step-by-step straight push of the rod. Smooth-rod caulking guns with a friction plate give a thrust ratio of about 10:1 or more; older notched ratchet-rod guns about 5:1 (Sashco). The rod runs along the gun or clamp, parallel to the push. Back-drivable: no while the holding plate is engaged.
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: tool-free quick release: press the release plate or lever and the rod slides out
- **seal**: none: nothing makes it seal
- **load**: Medium to heavy along the rod in the locking direction when hard steel plates bite a steel rod, bar or pipe (bar clamps, pipe clamps, caulking guns); zero in the free direction by design. Weak against twisting and sideways loads on the plate. The holding force depends on the rod surface: slick galvanized pipe, oily or glue-covered bars and worn plate edges slip (Sawmill Creek forum threads).
- **adjustable**: yes: stepless, so it holds at any point along the rod; the hold position is set simply by where you stop
- **play_and_precision**: This, plus the rod's springiness, is why caulk keeps oozing after you let go; drip-free guns deliberately let the rod retract a few millimetres when you press the release (Caulk Manufacturing). It tolerates a loose, rough fit: the hole only has to be loose enough for the plate to tip. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the plate is threaded onto the rod or bar from one end
- **materials**: Plates: hard steel, stamped from sheet, with a sharp sheared edge on one side of the hole that should face the direction it must bite (pipe-clamp and bar-clamp clutch plates, Sawmill Creek). Rods and bars: steel; black iron pipe grips better than galvanized pipe (Sawmill Creek). Housings and triggers: steel, cast metal or plastic. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: the idea is simple and works with a drilled steel plate or washer, a spring and a steel rod, but the biting parts should be metal, because a printed plastic plate or rod will wear and slip

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: salvage plates from caulking guns, pipe clamps or bar clamps, or buy replacement clutch plates
  - sheet metal: drill or punch a hole in steel plate, or use a steel washer
  - CNC or laser cut: steel plates with an accurate hole
  - 3D print (FDM): the housing, trigger, release lever and spring seats, not the biting plate
  - woodwork: frames and jaws for home-made clamps
- **print_in_place**: no: the biting plate and the rod should be steel and a spring is needed; only the housing and levers are printed
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Print the frame, trigger and release lever, but keep the biting plate and the rod metal (a steel washer or cut steel plate on a ground steel rod or flat bar). Put the sharp, sheared edge of the hole on the side that should bite (Sawmill Creek). Add a small compression spring to hold the plate tipped and a lever or button that pushes it square to release. Print the housing so the plate's reaction force presses into solid walls rather than pulling layers apart (FFF prints are stronger in one direction than another, Prusa), with thick walls or solid infill around the edge the plate leans on. Keep grease and oil off the rod: lubricant lowers friction and can stop it locking (Kalsi says to design with the lubricant that will be present). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Smooth-rod (friction-drive) caulking gun; one-handed trigger bar clamp, e.g. Quick-Grip (brand); pipe-clamp fixtures for black iron pipe (they hold with clutch plates); replacement 'clutch plates' for pipe and bar clamps; steel flat washers and fender washers; small compression springs; ground steel rod ('drill rod') or cold-rolled steel flat bar.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Binding threshold: a tipped part binds if the friction coefficient is equal to or greater than engagement length divided by engagement width, e.g. 0.8 in / 4.0 in = 0.2 (Kalsi Seals Handbook, ch. D21 'Sticky drawer effect'). A binding plate is deliberately made far thinner than this ratio needs, so it always locks.
  - The opposite rule for parts that must slide: keep length/diameter above 1, with 1.6:1 'very good' and 3:1 'awesome'; below 1, slides jam (Slocum, FUNdaMENTALS of Design, Topic 3, p. 3-8). A binding plate deliberately breaks this rule.
  - Plate square to the bar = slides; plate held at an angle that is 'transverse but not perpendicular' to the bar = binds (Sorensen and Gatzemeyer, US 4,926,722, quick-action bar clamp).
  - Caulking gun grips 'cramp the plunger shaft ... when canted relative to it, and ... release it when perpendicular to it' (Chang, US 4,081,112).
  - Fit clutch plates so the sharp edge of the hole bites into the pipe; reversing or re-sharpening worn plates can restore grip (Sawmill Creek forum threads).
  - Use black iron pipe rather than galvanized for pipe clamps; the galvanized coating is slick and slips (Sawmill Creek forum).
  - Caulking guns: smooth rod with friction plate about 10:1 thrust ratio or more; notched ratchet rod about 5:1 (Sashco).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Caulking gun: the trigger plate grabs and pushes the rod, and the thumb-release plate at the back stops it sliding back
  - One-handed trigger bar clamp: squeeze to walk the jaw along the bar; a braking plate holds it until you press the release lever
  - Pipe clamp: a stack of clutch plates on the sliding tail stop grips the pipe
  - Sliding-jaw F-style bar clamp: the jaw itself cocks slightly on the bar and holds by friction at the edges of its bar hole (Firgelli mechanism page)
  - Hunter's climbing tree stand, which uses the sticky drawer effect on purpose to lock onto the tree (Kalsi, ch. D21, footnote)
- **when_to_use**: When a rod, bar or pipe must hold at any point (not just at notches) against a push in one direction and release instantly by hand: clamps, pushers and feeders, adjustable stops and props, telescoping supports under light to medium loads, and simple trigger-driven straight-line drives. It works with cheap, rough parts; a steel washer on a steel rod will do.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it when the rod is plastic, oily, or slick-coated, because it slips: use a toothed linear ratchet instead. Avoid it for loads that must never drop, such as anything over people, unless it is a rated product: friction locks slip when surfaces are contaminated or edges wear, so use a positive lock (pin, toothed ratchet or screw). Avoid it where you need to hold both directions: use a clamp collar or set screw. Avoid it where you need exact, repeatable positions: use indexing holes or a detent.
- **environment_limits**: Anything that lowers friction can stop it locking: oil, grease, glue or sealant on the rod (Kalsi: the threshold must be checked with the lubricant present; Caulk Manufacturing on sealant residue on caulk-gun rods). New galvanized coatings are slick and slip until scored (Sawmill Creek). Rust and dirt change the grip and can stop it releasing smoothly. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Pinch points between clamp jaws and at the trigger. A clamp or caulk gun stores force, so releasing it under load can make the jaw or rod jump. If the plate slips (oily rod, worn edges) the load moves suddenly; never rely on a home-made friction lock to hold a person or a heavy load overhead. Stamped plates can have sharp edges when you take a tool apart.
- **common_failures**: Slipping: plate edges worn round, plates fitted backwards, weak spring, slick or galvanized bar, oily or glue-covered rod; fixes are to reverse, file or replace the plates so a sharp edge faces the load, fit a stronger spring, roughen or clean the bar, or switch to black pipe (Sawmill Creek). Rod won't retract or release smoothly: sealant or glue residue on the rod, or a burred rod (Caulk Manufacturing). Never locks: plate too thick for the rod or hole too tight to tip, so engagement length divided by width is higher than the friction coefficient (Kalsi). Rod marking: the biting edge scores the rod, so don't use it on finished surfaces.
- **related**: 
  - Linear ratchet (cable-tie ratchet): holds in fixed tooth steps with a positive, non-slipping lock instead of friction
  - Cam cleat and rope cam: a toothed or smooth cam wedges a rope instead of a plate wedging a rod
  - Wrap-spring clutch: the turning version of a self-tightening friction grip
  - Roller clutch and sprag clutch: turning one-way locks that wedge rollers or sprags instead of tipping a plate
  - Shaft collar or set screw: holds a rod in both directions but must be loosened with a tool to move
  - Screw clamp: slower, but holds in both directions and can be set to a known force
  - Collet or split-cone lock (telescoping poles): clamps all round by screwing a nut, holds both ways

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [US4926722A: Quick-action bar clamp (Sorensen and Gatzemeyer, 1990)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US4926722A/en): Patent drawings of a one-handed bar clamp with a trigger-tipped driving lever and a canted, spring-loaded braking lever on the slide bar *(licence: patent document; link only)*
  - [US2530359A: Operating mechanism for plungers of caulking guns (Peterson, 1950)](https://patents.google.com/patent/US2530359A/en): Drawings of a caulking gun's feeding plates and spring-loaded holding plate that bind on the plunger rod, with the finger release *(licence: patent document; link only)*
  - [Kalsi Seals Handbook, Chapter D21: Sticky drawer effect](https://www.kalsi.com/handbook/D21_Sticky_drawer_effect.pdf): Diagrams of engagement length versus width and of tipped parts binding at opposite corners (Figs. 1 and 3) *(licence: © Kalsi Engineering; link only)*
  - [Slocum, FUNdaMENTALS of Design, Topic 3 (p. 3-8, Saint-Venant's principle: bearings)](http://pergatory.mit.edu/resources/FUNdaMENTALs%20Book%20pdf/FUNdaMENTALs%20Topic%203.PDF): Sketches of slides that are stable, marginally stable, or jam, depending on their length-to-width ratio *(licence: © Alexander Slocum, free to read; link only)* *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **diagram_idea**: Side view of a rod through a plate with an exaggerated oversized hole: on the left the plate is square and slides (green arrow); on the right it is tipped a few degrees, with two red contact dots on opposite edges of the hole, a curved arrow showing the load tipping it harder, a small spring holding it tipped, and a thumb pressing a release lever to square it.

## 15 Bidirectional anti-back-drive clutch (reverse-input blocking clutch)

*self-locking & anti-back-drive.* A clutch that lets the input shaft turn the output either way, but locks if the output tries to turn the input, so a load can't drive the motor backwards

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A coupling that lets a motor or handle turn the output either way, but locks solid if the load tries to turn it back, so a raised arm, seat or blind stays put when you let go
- **term**: Bidirectional anti-back-drive clutch (reverse-input blocking clutch; non-back-drivable clutch)
- **aliases**: 
  - reverse-input blocking clutch
  - reverse input lock clutch / reverse input prevention clutch
  - anti-reverse input clutch
  - non-back-drivable clutch (NBDC) / non-back-drivable mechanism
  - irreversible roller clutch
  - back-drive prevention clutch
  - two-way anti-reverse transmission (Nguyen Duc Thang's animation title)
  - lock-type torque diode (NTN's product term)
  - seat lifter clutch
  - wrap clutch for anti-reverse transmission (window-blind version with a coil spring)
- **family**: self-locking & anti-back-drive
- **other_families**: clutches & couplings, one-way & ratchet, brakes, dampers & speed control
- **one_liner**: A 1:1 shaft coupling with wedging rollers (or a coil spring) that passes turning from the input to the output in both directions, but jams the output against a fixed housing whenever the output itself tries to turn, so a load cannot drive the motor or handle backwards.
- **complexity**: A small assembly that acts as one unit: a fixed outer ring or housing; an output hub with flat or ramped faces (a cam); rollers, balls or wedges sitting in wedge-shaped gaps between hub and ring; small springs pushing them into the gaps; an input cage or fork whose fingers sit between the rollers; and bearings (NTN catalogue; Thang's animations). The window-blind version uses a coil spring inside a fixed drum instead of rollers (EP 1 498 570).
- **how_it_works**: With nothing turning the input, springs hold the rollers lightly in wedge-shaped gaps between the output hub and the fixed housing, with gaps facing both ways, so if the load twists the output in either direction a roller jams and locks the output to the housing. When the input turns, fingers on its cage first push the rollers out of their wedges (unlocking), and after a small free angle the cage catches the output and drives it (NTN lock-type torque diode, Figs. 1-3). Thang describes it as having the self-locking of a worm drive but at a 1:1 ratio. In the coil-spring version, the load's twist expands the spring against a fixed drum so it grips, while turning the input releases the spring and drives through it (Nien Made, EP 1 498 570).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - stop it moving back (one-way): the load can never turn the input, whichever way it pushes
  - let it move (turn, slide or bend): the input still drives the output both ways
  - lock, and release by hand: with a hand crank or lever, working the input is what unlocks it
- **motion**: rotate: unlimited turning in both directions when driven from the input; locked when the output tries to drive
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the output turns about the shaft axis, but only when the input drives it; seen from the load side it has 0, because it is locked to the housing
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): the output jams against the fixed housing whichever way the load pushes
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): turning the input (crank, lever or motor) is the only way to release it
  - fail-safe or breakaway: it is passive, so a motor-driven load stays held when power is cut; Thang notes it lets a servo motor rest with its supply switched off
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): rollers or wedges jam by friction between the output hub and the fixed ring
  - spring force (a springy part presses it in place): small springs keep the rollers ready in their wedges
- **input_output**: Input rotation in either direction -> output rotation in the same direction at the same speed (1:1); shafts in line. Efficient, because nothing slides while it drives: about 0.95 for Controzzi's roller design, as cited by Liu et al. (2024), whereas Liu et al. list worm gears and high-ratio reducers as the usual, less attractive alternatives. Back-drivable: no, the output locks in both directions. A small free angle (lost motion) occurs each time the input changes direction, e.g. a 7° idle angle and 14° at the input in the Liu et al. prosthetic-wrist clutch. Variant: a 'free-type' unit lets the output spin freely instead of locking, but still never drives the input (NTN free-type torque diode).
- **power**: passive (no power needed); it holds the load whether or not the motor is powered, so if power is lost it stays locked
- **removability**: removable: it is a self-contained unit fitted between shaft ends and fixed to a frame
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: the unit can be unbolted and refitted; its life limit is wear of the rollers, ramps and springs
- **load**: Light to medium. Catalogue examples: 4.9 N·m allowed load torque (NTN lock-type torque diode TDL28), 29.4 N·m (swing-type TDS26), and 80 N·m braking force for a car seat-lifter clutch about 50 mm across (NTN TDLU); a prosthetic-wrist design locked about 0.6 N·m (Liu et al. 2024). It holds twist in both directions; bending and end loads must be carried by separate bearings.
- **adjustable**: no: how firmly it locks and how easily it unlocks are set by the design (roller size, ramp angle and springs)
- **play_and_precision**: Noticeable: there is a free angle every time the input changes direction, because the cage must push the rollers out before it catches the output (NTN; 7° idle and 14° input backlash in Liu et al.). Once locked it holds firmly, although the rollers wedge in a little further as load builds. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the unit fits between the ends of the input and output shafts
- **materials**: Hardened bearing-steel rollers and rings with spring-steel springs; a lightweight prosthetic version used 7075 aluminium for the main parts, stainless steel pins and wavy springs of 301 stainless steel (Liu et al. 2024). *(unverified parts omitted)*

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part: the usual choice (catalogue reverse-input blocking clutches, seat-lifter clutches, blind clutches)
  - CNC: hardened steel or aluminium rings, cam and cage for custom units, as in the Liu et al. prototype *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: no: the rollers should be steel pins and springs must be added, so the housing, cam and cage are printed as separate parts and assembled
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Treat a printed version as a light-duty demonstrator. Print the fixed ring, the output cam and the input cage lying flat, so the round and ramped faces are smooth vertical walls made of continuous perimeters. Leave at least 0.3 mm clearance between the cage and the parts it moves past (Prusa's starting value for moving parts). Keep each wedge gap shallow enough that friction locks it: the Liu et al. design needed a friction coefficient of about 0.25 between wedge and ring and suggests roughening the contact faces (for example by sandblasting) to raise friction. Give the cage a deliberate free angle so it pushes the rollers out of their wedges before it catches the output (NTN; Liu et al.). Push each roller into its wedge with a light spring; stronger springs raise the torque needed to unlock (Liu et al.). Expect printed plastic ramps to creep if a load hangs on them for hours (DuPont), so test with the real load overnight. Keep grease off the locking faces. For anything heavier than a demonstration, buy a unit or use a worm drive. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search terms: 'reverse input blocking clutch', 'reverse input lock clutch', 'anti-reverse input clutch', 'non-back-drivable clutch', 'lock-type torque diode' (NTN product name) and 'free-type torque diode' (output freewheels instead of locking), 'seat lifter clutch', 'bi-directional clutch', 'wrap spring clutch' (roller-blind and window-blind clutches). Alternatives: self-locking worm gearmotor, spring-applied (power-off) electromagnetic brake, one-way needle roller clutch (holds one direction only).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Catalogue ratings to compare against: NTN lock-type torque diode TDL28, 4.9 N·m allowed load torque; swing-type TDS26, 29.4 N·m; seat lifter clutch TDLU, 80 N·m braking force in a 50 mm diameter by 51 mm unit (NTN Clutches catalogue CAT. No. 2900, pp. 24-27).
  - Self-locking like a worm drive but at a 1:1 ratio; suited to motion systems where a servo motor should rest with its power switched off (Nguyen Duc Thang, 2700 Animated Mechanical Mechanisms, Part 1, p. 32).
  - Friction coefficient between wedge and fixed ring of about 0.25 preferred; unlocking torque about 65-85 N·mm and locking about 600 N·mm for a 33 mm diameter, 15 mm thick, 25.5 g clutch; 7° idle angle (Liu et al., Frontiers in Bioengineering and Biotechnology, 2024).
  - Roller-type non-back-drivable mechanisms can reach an efficiency of around 0.95 (Controzzi et al., as cited by Liu et al., 2024).
  - Self-locking (irreversibility) comes from friction combined with unfavourable transmission angles in the reverse direction; worm drives and power screws are the classic examples (Mecapedia, Irreversibilidad).
  - Expect a little backlash whenever the direction of rotation reverses (NTN, two-way clutch note, p. 23).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Car seat height lever: pump it up or down and the seat stays exactly where it is (NTN seat lifter clutch)
  - Window blinds worked by a crank or rod that stay where you leave them, because a wrap-spring anti-reverse clutch stops the blind's weight turning the mechanism (EP 1 498 570)
  - Hospital beds, car window regulators and powered shutters (applications listed in US 7,832,542)
  - Powered prosthetic hands and wrists that keep their grip with the motor off (Liu et al. 2024)
- **when_to_use**: When a motor or hand crank must move something both ways but the load must never drive it back: robot arm joints that should hold with power off, lifts and tilting tables, blinds and shutters, seat and bed adjusters, camera or antenna positioners. Choose it over a worm drive when you want a 1:1 drive with good efficiency (no big speed reduction); choose it over a power-off brake when you want holding without electronics or wiring.
- **when_not_to_use**: Not when the joint should give way when bumped, for safety or compliance: use a torque limiter or a back-drivable drive. Not for high torque or shock loads in a home-made printed version: buy a unit, or use a worm drive or power-off brake. Not where lost motion at each reversal matters for precise positioning: use a servo with a holding brake. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **environment_limits**: It holds by friction, so oil on the locking faces, wear or polished surfaces can let it slip (Liu et al. stress that the friction coefficient must meet a minimum); grease belongs only where the design intends it (Liu et al. grease the spring groove). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: If it slips from wear or oil, the load can drop, so never rely on a home-made one to hold anything above people. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: Slipping back under load: worn or polished rollers and ramps, oil on the faces, weak springs, or printed parts creeping; replace, clean, or roughen the surfaces (Liu et al. suggest sandblasting to raise friction). Hard to unlock, or the motor stalls when starting: springs too strong or wedges too steep; Liu et al. note the springs should be optimised to lower the unlocking torque. Wear of springs and cams in earlier designs (Liu et al., reviewing prior work). Contact faces deforming under overload (Liu et al. found failure when contact strain exceeded 0.2% in their aluminium wedges). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Worm drive (worm and worm wheel): also can't be back-driven, but reduces speed a lot and is less efficient
  - Lead screw and nut: self-locking straight-line version of the same idea
  - Roller clutch (one-way bearing) and sprag clutch: lock in one direction only and let the other freewheel
  - Free-type torque diode: the output can spin freely but still can't drive the input (NTN)
  - Wrap-spring clutch: the coil-spring version of the same wedging idea
  - Electromagnetic brake (power-off, spring-applied): holds with power off using springs and needs power to release
  - Ratchet and pawl: holds one way only, in tooth steps, with clicks
  - Binding-plate lock (tilting friction plate): a straight-line, one-direction friction lock
  - Torque limiter: the opposite goal; it lets go when overloaded

  - [NTN Clutches catalogue CAT. No. 2900, Section 3 Torque Diode (pp. 24-27)](https://www.ntnglobal.com/en/products/catalog/pdf/2900E.pdf): Cross-section drawings and three-step operating-principle figures of lock-type, free-type and swing-type torque diodes and the seat lifter clutch *(licence: © NTN; link only)* *(unverified parts omitted)*

## 16 Worm drive (worm and worm wheel)

*gear trains, gearboxes & transmissions.* A screw-like gear turning a gear wheel at right angles for a big speed reduction; many designs can't be turned backwards from the wheel (self-locking)

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A screw-shaped gear that turns a gear wheel beside it at right angles, slowly and with a lot of force; in many versions the wheel can't turn the screw back, so it stays where you left it
- **term**: Worm drive (worm gear pair: worm and worm wheel)
- **aliases**: 
  - worm gear
  - worm gearing
  - worm and wheel
  - worm and worm wheel
  - worm gear set
  - worm gearbox / worm gear reducer / right-angle worm gearbox
  - worm gear motor (a small motor with a worm stage)
  - endless screw (old name, used in 507 Mechanical Movements)
  - screw gear (loosely; strictly that name means crossed helical gears)
  - globoid or double-enveloping worm (variant with an hourglass-shaped worm)
  - duplex worm / dual-lead worm (variant whose backlash can be adjusted)
  - Schneckengetriebe (German)
- **family**: gear trains, gearboxes & transmissions
- **other_families**: self-locking & anti-back-drive
- **one_liner**: A screw-like gear (the worm) meshes with a gear wheel (the worm wheel) at right angles, giving a large speed reduction and turning-force increase in one step, and in low-angle, single-thread designs the wheel cannot drive the worm backwards.
- **complexity**: A few parts: the worm (a screw-shaped gear on the input shaft), the worm wheel (a gear wheel on the output shaft, often with curved 'throated' teeth that hug the worm), two shafts with bearings (including thrust bearings or washers, because the worm gets pushed along its own axis), and usually a housing that fixes the distance between the shafts and holds the grease or oil.
- **how_it_works**: The worm is a screw with one or more spiral threads (starts). Each full turn of a single-start worm pushes the wheel on by exactly one tooth, so a 20-tooth wheel gives a 20:1 reduction in a single mesh (ratio = wheel teeth divided by worm starts). The teeth slide across each other rather than rolling, which wastes energy as heat, and that same friction is why a worm with a shallow thread angle (lead angle) jams instead of turning when the wheel tries to drive it backwards (self-locking). Worms with more starts have a steeper thread, run more efficiently and can usually be driven backwards.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - change speed or turning force: a big reduction (typically 5:1 to 75:1, over 100:1 possible) in one small, right-angle step
  - stop it moving back (one-way): single-start, low-angle worms hold the output where it was left when the load tries to turn it back; note the worm itself can still be driven either way, and this hold is not guaranteed
- **motion**: rotate: continuous rotation in at the worm, slower continuous rotation out at the wheel, turned through 90 degrees; unlimited range, either direction
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: once the worm and wheel mesh, turning the worm is the only independent motion; the wheel's position is tied to it by the gear ratio
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): with a single-start worm and a small lead angle (about 4 degrees or less), friction stops the wheel from turning the worm, so a lifted load or a robot arm stays put when the motor stops. Makers warn it is not dependable: vibration, smooth ground worms, good lubrication or multi-start worms can let it slip, so add a brake where safety matters
- **holding_principle**: 
  - not a holding joint (it moves or passes on motion): it transmits rotation
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): its self-locking hold comes from sliding friction between the worm thread and the wheel teeth, not from the parts interlocking
- **input_output**: Continuous rotation -> slower continuous rotation. Ratio = number of wheel teeth / number of worm starts; normal single-stage range 5:1 to 75:1 (RoyMech) or 5:1 to 100:1 for worm gear reducers (KHK), over 100:1 possible in one stage (tec-science), and two worm reducers in series can reach 10,000:1 (KHK). Efficiency is poor because the teeth slide, and sources give different ranges: 30-90% in theory (KHK technical reference), typically 30-60% for cylindrical worms (KHK worm gear page), 60-95% for worm reducers depending on lead angle (KHK reducer page), and from about 98% at the lowest ratios down to about 20% at the highest (RoyMech). Compare 98-99.5% per mesh for spur and helical gears (KHK). Shafts: crossed (at right angles, not intersecting). Back-drivable: no for most single-start, high-ratio worms with a small lead angle (self-locking drives are usually under 50% efficient), but only reliably when there is no vibration; yes for multi-start, steep-lead worms (drives above about 50% efficiency are often not self-locking, per tec-science).
- **power**: passive (no power needed). When it is driven by a motor and the power is lost, a self-locking worm drive keeps its last position (the load cannot turn it back), while a back-drivable (multi-start) one lets the load turn the output
- **removability**: removable: the worm and wheel are separate parts fixed to their shafts (set screws, keys or clamps) inside a frame or housing and can be taken apart and refitted
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: it can be dismantled and reassembled repeatedly; the limit is tooth wear, not the assembly
- **tools_needed**: Hex keys or a screwdriver for set screws and housing screws; grease or oil for metal and most plastic pairs (self-lubricating plastic sets run dry); for a printed throated wheel split in two halves, glue or screws to join the halves
- **seal**: none: the gear mesh itself does not seal anything. Industrial worm gearboxes run in a closed housing that keeps the lubricant in and dirt out, so the housing (not the gears) does the sealing
- **load**: medium to heavy for its size, because the output gets a large torque multiplication. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **adjustable**: Not in normal running. Backlash (play) can be set by adjusting the distance between the shafts, or, with a duplex (dual-lead) worm, by sliding the worm along its axis without changing the centre distance (KHK quotes 0.02 mm backlash change per 1 mm of axial worm movement)
- **play_and_precision**: Small to noticeable backlash depending on quality; KHK advises keeping some backlash rather than zero, so an oil film can form. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: not applicable: it is a pair of gears mounted on two shafts inside a frame or housing, reached wherever the housing opens
- **materials**: Classic metal pair: a hardened (case-hardened and ground) steel worm with a phosphor-bronze wheel, the pairing RoyMech calls ideal; worms are made harder than wheels to limit wear (KHK). Other wheels: cast iron, aluminium bronze, acetal (POM) and nylon; stainless steel worms for wet places (KHK, McMaster-Carr). Grease-free self-lubricating plastic sets exist (igus iglide (brand)). Old wooden worm drives exist too (museum example at CNAM, Paris). For 3D printing, nylon is the usual first choice; PLA, ABS and PETG work only if greased (Let's Print test via Hackaday; EngineerDog ranks nylon > PLA > ABS > PETG for printed gears).
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: cheap to buy ready-made, and printable from free generators (BOSL2, wormgear), but the curved (throated) wheel is awkward to print because of overhangs, and the pair needs a rigid, accurate centre distance, a thrust bearing and grease to last

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part (worm gear sets, worm gear motors and worm gearboxes are cheap and common)
  - 3D print (FDM): demo and light-duty drives; best with nylon or greased PLA/PETG
  - CNC: worms are turned and thread-cut or hobbed; the wormgear tool offers a straight-flank (ZA) profile for CNC
  - woodwork: historic wooden worm drives exist (e.g. CNAM museum photo)
- **print_in_place**: yes, as a demo: Tomas Endlicher's 'Worm Gear Drive - Print In Place Test' prints already meshed with 0.4 mm clearances, but it needs supports and the moving parts must be carefully freed afterwards. Working drives are normally printed as separate parts and assembled on shafts.
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Don't model the teeth by hand: generate a matched pair with BOSL2 (worm() and worm_gear(), with backlash and clearance settings and a choice of starts and pressure angle) or the Python 'wormgear' tool (DIN 3975 geometry, reports efficiency and whether the pair will self-lock, and has a slightly convex ZK profile described as 3D-print-friendly). EngineerDog notes worms are hard to model and suggests starting from a template. 2) The throated worm wheel has overhangs on both faces: split it in half through its middle plane, print both halves flat without supports and join them (Libre Space community advice; DenverDude's 'Filament Spool Winder (Split Worm Gear)' prints flat with no supports this way). 4) Layers of 0.2 mm or finer, lots of part cooling and slow perimeters (about 25 mm/s) help the overhangs (Libre Space). 5) Leave clearance: the print-in-place test uses 0.4 mm under the wheel and 0.4-0.6 mm around the axles; in BOSL2 set a small backlash value. 6) Grease it: in Let's Print's test (reported by Hackaday), ungreased PLA, PETG and ABS worm sets failed in under two minutes at 8,000 rpm, while with plastic-safe grease each ran over ten minutes at 12,000 rpm (with almost no load, so treat this as a friction test only). 7) Material: nylon first (low friction, no grease needed, but absorbs water and is harder to print); PLA softens and deforms above about 60 °C (Prusa), and the sliding friction plus a nearby powered stepper motor can get it that hot (Libre Space). 8) EngineerDog suggests a 25-degree pressure angle for stronger printed gear teeth in general, and oversizing shaft holes slightly (about 0.005 in on the diameter) or reaming them. 9) Hold the two shafts rigidly at the right distance and add a thrust washer or bearing at the worm's ends, because the worm is pushed along its axis. 10) Don't count on a printed worm to self-lock under vibration; test it with the real load. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Worm and worm gear sets (specified by module or diametral pitch, number of starts, hand and pressure angle, typically 14.5 or 20 degrees); shaft-mounted worms; worm gears (wheels) in bronze, cast iron, acetal or nylon (McMaster-Carr 'Metal Worms and Worm Gears' and 'Plastic Worm Gears'); worm gear motors (small DC motors with a worm stage, e.g. the 'JGY-370' type seen on Printables); worm gear reducers or right-angle worm gearboxes; grease-free plastic modular worm gears (igus Apiro (brand), ratios 4:1, 32:1, 64:1); LEGO Technic worm gears (brand) for quick experiments; thrust bearings and thrust washers for the worm shaft.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Ratio = number of wheel teeth / number of worm starts; a single-start worm on a 20-tooth wheel gives 20:1 (Wikipedia 'Worm drive'; tec-science). Worms usually have 1 to 4 starts (tec-science).
  - Normal single-stage range 5:1 to 75:1 (RoyMech); 5:1 to 100:1 for worm gear reducers, and 10,000:1 with two in series (KHK reducer page); KHK stock sets go up to 120:1 (KHK worm gear page).
  - Efficiency: 30-90% theoretical (KHK technical reference), typically 30-60% for cylindrical worms (KHK worm gear page), 60-95% for worm reducers (KHK reducer page); about 98% at the lowest ratios down to about 20% at the highest (RoyMech). Higher ratio and smaller lead angle mean lower efficiency, and phosphor-bronze wheels are more efficient than aluminium-bronze or cast-iron ones (KHK Q&A).
  - Self-locking happens when the lead angle is about 4 degrees or less, but it is unstable because it depends on friction (KHK Q&A). The limit is where the lead angle equals arctan(friction coefficient); use the lower, moving (dynamic) friction value if there is any vibration (RoyMech).
  - Self-locking worm drives are usually below 50% efficient; above 50% they are often not self-locking (tec-science).
  - Don't use a worm as the braking device for safety-critical lifting such as hoists (RoyMech); combine self-locking with another method when reversal must be prevented completely (KHK).
  - Make the worm harder than the wheel; a case-hardened, ground steel worm with a phosphor-bronze wheel is the ideal metal pair (RoyMech; KHK).
  - Keep some backlash; zero backlash is not recommended because it disrupts the oil film and can cause abnormal wear and jamming (KHK worm gear page and Q&A).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Guitar and double-bass tuning pegs: turning the small knob slowly turns the string post, and the string's pull can't unwind it
  - Worm-drive hose clamps (Jubilee clip (brand)): a small screw drives the slotted band (the worm here acts on a straight band rather than a wheel)
  - Gate openers, lifts and conveyor drives
  - Car steering boxes (and, historically, some truck rear axles)
- **when_to_use**: Use a worm drive when you need a big slow-down (or a big increase in turning force) in a small space, when the output shaft has to be at right angles to the motor, or when the output should stay put when the motor stops, such as a robot arm joint, a tilting camera or tablet stand, a winch that holds its load, or a rotating table. It is also quiet and smooth, and a single stage replaces several spur gear stages.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it when efficiency matters (battery robots, continuous high-power drives), because a self-locking worm is usually under 50% efficient, so more than half the input turns into heat (tec-science); use a spur, helical or planetary gearbox instead. Avoid it when you need to back-drive the output by hand or let it give way safely, as with compliant robots, things people push, or mechanisms that must not be damaged by knocks; use a back-drivable gearbox with a clutch. Don't rely on it as the only brake for lifting people or heavy loads; add a real brake or a ratchet and pawl. For straight-line motion, use a lead screw or rack and pinion.
- **environment_limits**: Heat: sliding friction makes heat, and metal pairs need an unbroken oil film (KHK); PLA softens and deforms above about 60 °C (Prusa), and a printed worm next to a powered stepper motor can reach that (Libre Space community). igus's grease-free plastic modular worm gear is rated for +5 to +50 °C (igus). Vibration: shocks and vibration can undo static self-locking and let the gearbox start turning under the load (tec-science; RoyMech). Dirt: fully plastic, self-lubricating sets are described as resistant to dirt and dust (igus); greased sets pick up grit.
- **safety_notes**: Self-locking is not a safety brake: a load can creep or drop under vibration, so never stand under a load held only by a worm drive (RoyMech; KHK). Worm gearboxes can get hot in continuous use. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: 1) Worn or stripped wheel teeth, since the wheel is the softer part: make it from bronze or nylon, keep it lubricated and don't overload it. 2) Unlubricated plastic worms wear out in minutes: grease them with a plastic-safe grease (Let's Print via Hackaday). 3) Overheating and softening from friction: lower the speed, use a multi-start worm or a better material. 4) Unexpected back-driving under vibration or with a multi-start worm: check the lead angle and add a brake. 5) Binding or rapid wear from zero backlash or a wrong centre distance (KHK). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Crossed helical gears (screw gears): also crossed shafts but with both gears helical; 70-95% efficient (KHK) and not suited to high power
  - Bevel gears: turn motion through 90 degrees with the shafts intersecting; 98-99% efficient per mesh (KHK) and used for low ratios
  - Lead screw and nut: the same screw-and-friction idea, but the screw drives a nut in a straight line instead of a wheel
  - Worm gear screw jack: a worm turning a nut or screw to lift loads, combining both
  - Rack and pinion: turns rotation into straight-line motion
  - Planetary or cycloidal (Cyclo) reducer: a large ratio in line with the motor shaft, about 98% efficient for one stage (KHK) and better at taking shock loads than a worm reducer (KHK)
  - Ratchet and pawl or a brake: what to add when the output must never move back *(unverified parts omitted)*

### Pictures, videos and models

  - [Filament Spool Winder (Split Worm Gear) (DenverDude, Printables)](https://www.printables.com/model/642115-filament-spool-winder-split-worm-gear): A working worm-driven winder whose worm wheel is split in two so it prints flat with no supports *(licence: CC BY-NC)*

## 17 Lead screw and nut (incl. anti-backlash nut)

*motion conversion.* A threaded rod turning inside a nut to push something in a straight line; low-friction versions can be back-driven, fine-pitch ones hold their place

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A long threaded rod that you (or a motor) turn so a nut riding on it moves in a straight line; with a gentle, fine thread it stays where it is when you let go
- **term**: Lead screw and nut (power screw, translation screw), including the anti-backlash nut
- **aliases**: 
  - leadscrew
  - power screw
  - translation screw
  - Acme screw / Acme thread (inch sizes)
  - trapezoidal screw / Tr screw (metric), e.g. 'T8' or 'Tr8x8' lead screw on 3D printers
  - jackscrew / screw jack (when used for lifting)
  - feed screw (machine tools)
  - vice screw / bench screw
  - high-helix lead screw (steep, fast, back-drivable version)
  - anti-backlash nut / zero-backlash nut / preload nut / spring-loaded nut
  - dryspin (igus brand of plastic lead screw nuts)
  - OpenBuilds Anti-Backlash Nut Block (brand)
- **family**: motion conversion
- **other_families**: sliding, telescoping & linear motion, self-locking & anti-back-drive
- **one_liner**: Turning a threaded rod (the lead screw) moves a nut that is kept from spinning along the rod in a straight line, trading speed for a lot of pushing force; fine threads hold their place, and a spring-loaded anti-backlash nut removes the wobble when the direction changes.
- **complexity**: A few parts: the threaded rod (screw), the nut (plastic, brass or bronze), bearings or supports at the rod's ends, a handwheel or a motor coupling, and a guide (rails or slides) that stops the nut and carriage from turning with the screw. An anti-backlash nut is a small assembly: two nut parts (or a nut with springy fingers) plus a compression or torsion spring.
- **how_it_works**: The nut is held so it can't turn, so each turn of the screw moves it along by one lead (the distance one thread advances per turn = pitch x number of starts). Because the threads slide on each other, friction is high: with a shallow thread (small lead angle) the friction is greater than the thread's slope, so pushing on the nut cannot spin the screw (self-locking), while steep, multi-start threads are efficient enough that a load can spin the screw backwards (back-drive). There is always a small gap between the nut's and screw's thread faces (backlash), so the nut shifts a little when the direction reverses. An anti-backlash nut splits the nut in two and uses a spring to push the halves apart along the screw, or to squeeze springy fingers inward, so one part always presses on each thread face.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - change the kind of motion: rotation into straight-line motion (and back again if it is back-drivable)
  - change speed or turning force: a small turning force makes a large push, at slow speed
  - stop it moving back (one-way): fine-lead screws hold their position when you stop turning (self-locking); note it can still be driven both ways from the screw, and the hold is not guaranteed under vibration
- **motion**: rotate+slide: the screw turns and the nut slides in a straight line; travel is limited by the length of the screw
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the nut's travel is locked to the screw's rotation (one lead per turn), so there is only one independent motion
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - motion conversion (rotary to linear): the screw's rotation becomes a straight push or pull on the nut
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): true only for fine leads (a rule of thumb is a lead under about 1/3 of the screw diameter, efficiency under 50%); vibration can let even these creep, so add a brake for hanging loads
  - tensioning (takes up slack and holds), anti-backlash nut only: its spring keeps taking up the gap between the threads as the nut wears
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (form-fit): the nut's thread interlocks with the screw's thread, so the nut can't slide along the screw without turning it
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): thread friction is what makes a fine-lead screw self-locking
  - spring force: in anti-backlash nuts, a spring presses the nut parts against opposite thread faces
- **input_output**: Rotation -> straight-line motion. Travel per turn = lead (e.g. a Tr8x8 3D-printer screw moves 8 mm per turn; a Tr8x2 moves 2 mm). Efficiency typically 20-80% (PBC Linear; linearmotiontips), 30-50% for jackscrews (Wikipedia); ball screws, by contrast, are about 90%. Shaft arrangement: in line (the nut travels along the screw's axis). Back-drivable: yes when the efficiency is 50% or more, e.g. a 10 mm screw with a 25 mm lead (83%); no when the lead is under about 1/3 of the diameter, e.g. a 10 mm screw with a 2 mm lead (41%) (PBC Linear); vibration can still make a self-locking screw move.
- **power**: passive (no power needed); often motor-driven. If power is lost, a self-locking (fine-lead) screw keeps its last position, while a back-drivable (steep-lead) screw can let a vertical load, such as a 3D printer gantry or bed, drift or drop unless there is a brake or counterweight
- **removability**: removable: the nut unscrews from the end of the screw, and the screw comes out of its end bearings and coupling
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: it can be dismantled and refitted repeatedly; the nut wears in use and is replaceable
- **tools_needed**: Hex keys or screwdrivers for the nut-mounting screws, bearing clamps and coupler set screws; grease for brass and bronze nuts (self-lubricating plastic nuts run dry); a tap (M5 in the OpenBuilds-style printable block) if you print your own nut block
- **seal**: none: the threads and nut are open to the air and dirt; nothing about the joint seals
- **load**: Medium to heavy along the screw's axis in both push and pull (screw jacks lift cars and buildings). Weak against sideways loads and bending: the nut should ride on a separate guide, not carry side loads. Long, thin screws whip (vibrate like a skipping rope) at speed, so the safe speed limit falls as length increases.
- **adjustable**: yes: the position can be set anywhere along the travel just by turning the screw. Anti-backlash nuts have their preload set by a spring (automatic, e.g. torsion-spring twin nuts) or an adjusting screw (e.g. the M5 screw on the OpenBuilds-style block, tightened until the play is gone but the screw still turns easily)
- **play_and_precision**: Plain nut: noticeable backlash (the gap between thread faces), which grows as the nut wears. Anti-backlash nut: small to none; Sienci Labs' spring-loaded POM nut takes up wear automatically. Fine leads give fine position steps, and repeatability is best when the nut approaches a position from the same direction. It tolerates little misalignment: a screw held by the motor coupler plus a nut on a guided carriage is over-constrained, so any bend or offset causes binding and wobble. Let the float happen in the nut mount or guides, not the screw (igus).
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the nut is threaded on from an end of the screw, and the screw is fitted from its ends into end bearings and a motor coupling or handwheel
- **materials**: Screw: steel or stainless steel (sometimes brass or aluminium). Nut: bronze (lowest friction of the metals), brass, cast iron, or plastics such as POM (Delrin/acetal) and igus iglide (brand). Thread friction coefficient ranges from about 0.04 to 0.25 depending on the pair and lubrication (Wikipedia). Printed nuts: PLA works, ABS is a bit worse, and Delrin would be ideal (KP's printable OpenBuilds block); nuts printed in nylon and POM were not as good as machined ones (0scar).
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to fit if bought (T8 lead screw and brass nut sets are cheap 3D-printer parts); moderate to print: coarse trapezoidal or buttress threads for jacks and vises print well, but precise small nuts and anti-backlash nuts print worse than machined ones

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part (by far the most common: Acme or trapezoidal screws with brass, bronze or plastic nuts)
  - 3D print (FDM): nuts and nut blocks for metal screws, and coarse printed screws for jacks, vises and clamps
  - CNC: threads cut on a lathe (screw-cutting) or thread-milled
- **print_in_place**: no: the screw and nut are printed separately and screwed together, or a printed nut is used on a metal screw
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: 1) Usually buy the screw and print only the nut holder or nut: a metal Tr8 screw with a brass or POM nut beats a printed screw for precision. 2) Printable nut block (KP's printable version of the OpenBuilds anti-backlash block): 0.4 mm nozzle, 0.2 mm layers, 5 perimeters, 5 solid top and bottom layers, no supports; screw the lead screw in and out a few times to loosen the fit, add grease, then tap the M5 adjusting hole and tighten it gently until the play is gone but the screw still turns easily. Use something hard, low-friction and slow-wearing: PLA works, ABS is a bit worse, Delrin would be ideal. 3) Fine layers make smoother threads: 0scar printed his OpenSCAD anti-backlash nut at 0.12 mm layers (it came out a bit tight) and found nylon and POM prints worse than machined nuts, so treat printed anti-backlash nuts as experiments. 4) For printed screws, a buttress-style profile with a flat load face and a 45-degree back face prints without support if the screw stands upright with the flat faces pointing up. Miloslav Brozek's parametric screw jack (Printables model 1724067, read via the Printables API) uses 0.16-0.2 mm layers, at least 3 walls, and about 0.5 mm radial and 0.4 mm axial clearance for a free-spinning fit that tightens up under load. 5) Generate threads with BOSL2's trapezoidal_threaded_rod()/trapezoidal_threaded_nut() or acme_threaded_rod()/acme_threaded_nut(); its $slop setting adds printer clearance to internal threads, and 'starts' sets multi-start leads. 6) Mount the nut so it can float slightly sideways, or it will fight the guide rails and bind (igus). 7) PLA softens above about 60 °C (Prusa), so keep printed nuts away from motors and heated beds, and don't lift anything dangerous with a printed jack (nightfox90's own warning).
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Lead screws and nuts in Acme, rounded Acme and metric trapezoidal threads, and 'precision lead screws and nuts' for motor-driven motion (McMaster-Carr); lead screw end supports and collars; T8 (Tr8x8 4-start, Tr8x2) lead screws with brass or POM flange nuts, sold as 3D-printer and CNC parts; anti-backlash nuts: spring-loaded split nuts, twin nuts with a torsion spring (igus zero-backlash and anti-backlash nuts), and adjustable anti-backlash nut blocks (OpenBuilds (brand)); self-lubricating plastic nuts and high-helix screws (igus dryspin (brand)); flexible shaft couplings for the motor; complete lead-screw linear actuator kits (ServoCity (brand), popular in FTC robotics per Game Manual 0).
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Travel per turn = lead = pitch x number of starts; a thread marked 'Tr 60x18(P9)' has an 18 mm lead, a 9 mm pitch and therefore 2 starts (Wikipedia 'Trapezoidal thread form'). So Tr8x8 (P2) is 4-start, moving 8 mm per turn.
  - A screw is self-locking when the friction coefficient is greater than the tangent of the lead angle, but vibration can undo it (Wikipedia 'Leadscrew').
  - Lead screws with 50% or more efficiency will back-drive; if the lead is less than 1/3 of the screw's diameter it won't (PBC Linear; linearmotiontips also gives the 1/3 rule). Example: 10 mm screw, 2 mm lead = 41% efficient (holds); 25 mm lead = 83% (back-drives) (PBC Linear).
  - Efficiency: lead screws about 20-80%, ball screws about 90% (PBC Linear; linearmotiontips); a steeper lead angle is more efficient (Roton: 8 degrees gives 45% forward and -13% backward efficiency, i.e. self-locking; 20 degrees gives 65% forward and 52% backward).
  - Duty cycle: plastic nuts about 50% under rated load, bronze nuts as low as 10% (linearmotiontips).
  - Keep the running speed below about 80% of the screw's critical (whipping) speed, which falls with length and depends on the end supports (Wikipedia 'Leadscrew'). To cure whipping, use a thicker or shorter screw or a lower speed (igus).
  - Support the screw ends firmly (bearings plus clamps); loose end support is only for slow, hand-driven screws; put any needed float in the guides or nut mount, not the screw (igus Engineer's Toolbox).
  - Plastic nut limits: maximum surface pressure about 4 MPa for iglidur J, 5 MPa for W300 and 3.5 MPa for A180; iglidur nuts work from -20 to +150 °C (igus lead screw FAQ).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A bench vice: turning the handle drives the jaw with great force, and it doesn't loosen by itself
  - A car scissor jack: turning the crank winds a screw that lifts the car and holds it (a jackscrew)
  - The Z axis of a 3D printer: motors turn T8 lead screws that raise the print head or lower the bed
  - The laser head in a DVD drive or the head of an old floppy drive, moved along a tiny lead screw by a stepper motor
- **when_to_use**: Use one when you need slow, strong, precise straight-line motion from a motor or a handwheel: lifting and holding (jacks, printer Z axes, robot hanging mechanisms), clamping (vices, presses), fine positioning (machine tool slides, CNC axes), or opening and closing things that must stay put with the power off. It is cheap, compact, quiet, works with plastic nuts that need no grease, and a fine lead holds position without a brake.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it for fast or long moves (it is slow, and long screws whip at speed); use a belt, rack and pinion or linear motor. Avoid it for continuous high-duty or high-efficiency work (it wastes energy as heat and the nut wears); use a ball screw. Don't rely on its self-locking to hold heavy vertical loads safely; add a brake or counterweight. Don't use it where sideways loads would hit the nut; add linear guides. For quick coarse moves followed by fine clamping, add a half nut or use a quick-release mechanism.
- **environment_limits**: Vibration can let a self-locking screw creep (Wikipedia; PBC Linear). Lubrication and wear matter over time: worn nut threads raise backlash, and in aircraft jackscrews poor maintenance has been fatal (Wikipedia 'Jackscrew', Alaska Airlines Flight 261). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: A back-drivable (steep-lead or low-friction) screw can let a raised load, printer bed or gantry drop when the motor is switched off; use a fine lead, a brake or a counterweight (linearmotiontips). Screw jacks are only safe while the nut threads are in good condition and lubricated (Wikipedia 'Jackscrew'). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: 1) Nut wear, so backlash grows: use an anti-backlash nut, bronze or POM nuts, and grease metal nuts. 2) Binding and wobble from misalignment or over-constraint: align the screw with the guides and let the nut float slightly (igus). 3) Whipping and noise at high speed: use a shorter or thicker screw, better end supports or a lower speed (igus; Wikipedia). 4) Load dropping when unpowered with a steep lead: switch to a finer lead or add a brake. 5) Anti-backlash nut problems: over-adjusting or wrong assembly binds the screw, and uneven spring forces cause concentrated wear (Sienci Labs); springs that are too weak leave play. 6) Printed nuts coming out too tight: print finer and run the screw in and out to wear the nut in (KP; 0scar).
- **related**: 
  - Ball screw: balls roll between screw and nut, about 90% efficient, fast and precise, but back-drives easily (about 80% backward efficiency per Roton) and costs more
  - Threadless ball screw (rolling-ring drive): tilted bearings on a smooth rod act as the nut; slips instead of jamming when overloaded
  - Half nut (split nut): a nut that can be opened off the screw for quick free sliding, then closed to drive again
  - Worm drive: the same screw friction, but the screw drives a gear wheel instead of a nut
  - Rack and pinion: a gear on a toothed bar; faster, efficient and back-drivable, but doesn't hold position by itself
  - Belt drive (timing belt): fast, long, cheap linear motion with lower force and no self-locking
  - Differential screw: two threads of slightly different pitch for extremely fine adjustment
  - Roller screw: threaded rollers instead of balls, for very high loads

  - [HUANGYAN920 Mechanical: interactive animations (entries 'SCREW-NUT', 'ACME-SCREW', 'SCREW-JACK', 'FEED-SCREW')](https://huangyan920.github.io/Mechanical/): Adjustable browser animations of a screw driving a nut, a screw jack and a machine feed screw *(licence: MIT)*
