## 22 Eccentric cam lever (cam clamp, quick-release skewer)

*over-centre, toggle & cam clamps.* A lever with an off-centre cam that squeezes or clamps when flipped over and releases when flipped back, as on bike wheel skewers and seat posts

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A lever with a lopsided round end: flip it one way and the off-centre bulge presses the parts together hard and stays shut; flip it back and they come free, like the lever on a bike wheel or seat post
- **term**: Eccentric cam lever (cam clamp, quick-release skewer)
- **aliases**: 
  - cam lever
  - cam clamp
  - eccentric clamp
  - eccentric lever
  - clamping lever with eccentric cam (industrial standard-part name, e.g. GN 927)
  - cam-action clamp
  - quick-release lever or QR lever
  - quick-release skewer (bicycle wheels)
  - quick-release seatpost clamp (bicycle seat posts and stems)
  - lever lock or flick lock (trekking poles, tripod legs)
  - cam lock lever (not the furniture 'cam lock' fitting, which is a different part)
  - internal-cam and external-cam skewer
  - spiral cam clamp (a variant with a spiral rather than circular cam)
  - Exzenterspanner or Schnellspanner (German names)
- **family**: over-centre, toggle & cam clamps
- **other_families**: quick-release & tool-free, clamping, magnetic & friction connections, self-locking & anti-back-drive
- **one_liner**: A hand lever whose pivot is set off-centre in a round or spiral cam, so swinging the lever wedges the cam against a surface and squeezes parts together with many times the force of your hand, and the cam's gentle slope (or a small over-centre step) keeps it shut until you flip it back.
- **complexity**: A few parts: lever with the cam shape (or a cam body with a lever), a pivot pin or axle, a bearing surface or washer the cam presses on, and usually a threaded rod or stud with an adjusting nut that sets how tight it closes. A bike quick-release skewer is: lever and cam, cam body, skewer rod, two conical springs and an adjusting nut (Sheldon Brown).
- **how_it_works**: The lever turns a cam whose pivot is off-centre (eccentric), so as the lever swings, the distance from the pivot to the pressing face grows, like pushing a slowly thickening wedge into a gap (Firgelli, Cam clamp; Ganter). A long lever and a small cam rise turn a modest hand push into a large clamping force, roughly 17 to 25 times the hand force on standard industrial cam levers (Ganter table). It stays closed because the cam slope at the closed position is shallower than the friction angle, so friction stops it sliding back (self-locking), and many levers, such as bike skewers, go slightly past the highest point of the cam ('over the top') so that vibration pushes them further closed rather than open (Sheldon Brown). The threaded rod and adjusting nut set the starting gap, and so how hard it clamps.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: join and hold: clamps a wheel in a frame, a seat post in a tube, a part in a jig or a bracket on a rail, lock, and release by hand: flips shut to hold and flips open to release, without tools
- **motion**: rotate: the lever swings through part of a turn (about 90 to 180 degrees) to produce a short straight pull or push of about a millimetre or two; industrial spiral cams lock anywhere within a 180-degree throw (Carr Lane)
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1 while operating: the lever turns about its pivot. Once closed, the clamped parts have 0, because friction holds them together
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - self-locking (holds under load, won't back-drive): a shallow cam slope, smaller than the friction angle, stops the clamping force from pushing the lever open (Firgelli; Ganter: self-locking 'as soon as the dead centre is exceeded')
  - over-centre (snaps past a point and stays): bike skewer cams go 'over the top' so the clamp loosens very slightly as the lever reaches the end, and vibration then tends to hold it closed (Sheldon Brown)
  - quick-release: one flick of the lever opens or closes it with no tools, which is why Tullio Campagnolo invented it for racing wheels in 1927 (Wikipedia, Quick release skewer)
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): the clamped load cannot open it; only moving the lever does
- **holding_principle**: 
  - friction or clamping (force-fit): the parts are held by being squeezed together; the cam itself is held shut by friction on its shallow slope
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): only for over-centre cams, where the lever must climb back over a small bump in the cam profile to open
- **input_output**: Force gain about 17 to 25 times: e.g. 75 N at the end of a 44 mm lever gives 1,250 to 1,750 N, and 350 N on a 101 mm lever gives 6,100 to 8,000 N (Ganter). In-line arrangement: the lever usually pulls on a rod or stud through its own pivot. Back-drivable: no when correctly designed (self-locking or over-centre), but eccentric cams can shake loose under strong vibration (Carr Lane). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: tool-free quick release: flipping the lever releases the clamp by hand; setting the tightness once needs only finger-turning of the adjusting nut or knob
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: made to be opened and closed daily (bike wheels, jigs, poles); cams and washers slowly wear, and printed plastic cams creep
- **seal**: none: it only clamps; internal-cam skewers enclose the cam, which keeps dirt out of the mechanism but seals nothing (Sheldon Brown)
- **load**: Strong in straight pull along the rod; the joint it makes resists sliding (shear) only by friction, so a sideways or twisting load, such as disc-brake forces on a wheel, can make it slip or loosen (Wikipedia, Sheldon Brown) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **adjustable**: yes: the adjusting nut or threaded stud sets the starting gap and so the clamping force (Sheldon Brown); industrial levers offer an adjustable bearing plate (JW Winco); some trekking-pole lever locks have a tension dial for adjusting on the go (MSR)
- **play_and_precision**: Play: none once clamped (held by friction). Repeatability: the clamp force repeats as long as the adjusting nut is not moved; position repeats only as well as whatever locating features the clamped parts have. Misalignment: tolerates small errors because it just squeezes, but the cam must bear squarely on its washer; on external-cam skewers a curved plastic washer can turn 90 degrees so the cam presses on the wrong face and the clamp goes dangerously loose (Sheldon Brown)
- **access_needed**: both sides for through-rod types (lever on one end, nut on the other, as on bike skewers); one side only (blind) when the lever's stud screws into a tapped hole
- **materials**: Steel cam in a metal body (internal-cam skewers), aluminium levers and cams (external-cam skewers) with plastic, rubber or brass washers (Sheldon Brown, Brainy Biker); industrial levers in plastic, polyamide (PA), steel, zinc die-cast or stainless steel (JW Winco); aluminium lever and stainless steel cam on trekking-pole locks (MSR); hardwood (e.g. maple) for woodworking cam clamps, often with cork, urethane or knurled pads for grip (Firgelli). Printed versions in PLA or PETG around a steel bolt (Printables)
- **maker_difficulty**: easy to moderate: a printed cam lever around a bolt or a store-bought cam lever is easy to fit, but getting a cam that clamps hard, stays shut and doesn't creep open needs a shallow cam slope, a metal pivot and a tough plastic

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part (clamping levers with eccentric cam, cam handles, bike quick-release skewers)
  - 3D print (FDM): cam levers and clips around a metal bolt or pin; print-in-place bag clips
  - woodwork: traditional hardwood cam clamps and jig hold-downs
  - CNC: machined metal cams and levers for heavier clamping *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: partly: most printed cam levers need an added bolt or pin as the pivot and a nut or threaded stud to pull on (e.g. mikan's cam lever needs an M3 or M4 bolt); small light-duty designs such as Andrei's bag clip print fully assembled as three hinged parts (Printables)
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Make the cam slope at the closed position shallower than the slip (friction) angle of your plastic: Andrei's print-in-place bag clip uses a quick-closing first quarter turn, then a quarter turn with a 10-degree slope, measured to be below the slip angle for PLA, which gives the most force and cannot back-drive, and ends with a short constant-radius section that gives a satisfying lock (Printables 27216). His clip has 0.1 mm clearance on the printed shaft (with 0.15, 0.20 and 0.30 mm versions for looser printers) and 0.15 mm layers because the vertical gaps are exactly three layers; other layer heights change the gaps. Use a steel bolt or pin as the pivot and a washer under the cam, as in the CNC side clamp, which uses an M6 bolt and a 6 x 25 mm washer, PETG, 6 walls, 25 percent gyroid infill, and is printed on its side for strength, not flat (Printables 1676459). Walls matter more than infill for printed clamps (3 shells recommended, Hackaday.io bar clamps). Expect printed cams to lose some clamp force over time, because plastic under constant load creeps and PLA makes a poor spring (Hackaday, 'The Challenges Of 3D Printing Reliable Springs'); *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search: 'clamping lever with eccentric cam' (e.g. GN 927 series, lever lengths 44 to 101 mm, threads M3 to M12 or 8-32 to 3/8-16, tapped or threaded-stud versions, with or without adjustable bearing plate; JW Winco/Ganter), 'cam handle' or 'cam lever' (Carr Lane), 'cam edge clamp', 'cam action strap clamp', 'spiral cam clamp', 'quick release skewer' (internal cam or external cam; front and rear lengths), 'quick release seatpost clamp', 'lever lock' or 'flick lock' spare parts for trekking poles and tripods.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Self-locking needs the cam rise angle to be no bigger than the friction angle, phi = atan(mu); for steel on dry hardwood (mu about 0.3) keep the profile within about 17 degrees of the tangent at the lock point (Firgelli, Cam clamp)
  - A 10-degree cam slope was measured to be below the slip angle for printed PLA on PLA and does not back-drive (Andrei, Bag Clip (PRINT IN PLACE CAM), Printables)
  - Standard eccentric clamping levers: 44 mm lever and 75 N hand force give 1,250 to 1,750 N; 63 mm and 125 N give 2,250 to 3,100 N; 82 mm and 200 N give 3,700 to 5,000 N; 101 mm and 350 N give 6,100 to 8,000 N, i.e. a force gain of about 17 to 25 times (Ganter, 'Clamping and manual forces in clamping levers with eccentrical cam')
  - Friction coefficients to assume: plastic on plastic about 0.25, steel on steel about 0.2 (0.1 lubricated), plastic on steel about 0.15; use safety factors of 1.2 to 1.5 for steady loads, 1.8 to 2.4 for pulsating and 3 to 4 for alternating loads (Ganter)
  - Clamping force is roughly hand force x lever length / [cam lever arm x (wedge term + friction at the cam) + pivot radius x friction at the pivot], so friction at the pivot and cam eats a large share of the force (Ganter worked example: 350 N x 76 mm gives about 7,000 N)
  - A circular eccentric has only one true locking point; a spiral (involute) cam locks anywhere over its throw, but both can loosen under heavy vibration, so use them for drill jigs, light-to-medium milling, inspection and assembly fixtures where vibration is small (Carr Lane)
  - Bike skewer setup: turn the adjusting nut until the gap closes with the lever sticking out at about 70 degrees, then press the lever home; it should take enough force to leave an imprint on your palm (Sheldon Brown; Wikipedia)
  - Internal (enclosed) cams clamp harder than external cams, especially cheap external cams with a compressible plastic washer (Sheldon Brown, BikeRadar, Brainy Biker)

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Quick-release skewers on bicycle wheels (Wikipedia, Sheldon Brown)
  - Quick-release seat post and stem clamps on bicycles (Sheldon Brown)
  - Lever locks on adjustable trekking poles and tripod legs (MSR)
  - Sash window locks, where a cam pulls the window shut and locks itself (Wikipedia, Cam (mechanism))
- **when_to_use**: Use a cam lever when something must be clamped firmly but released and re-clamped often and fast, with no tools and even with cold or gloved hands: wheels and seat posts, adjustable poles and tripod legs, fences and stops on woodworking jigs, work-holding in drill jigs and assembly fixtures, and brackets that slide on aluminium extrusion. It is also a neat way for makers to add a strong, adjustable clamp using one bolt and a printed lever.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it where there is strong vibration or shock (use a screw clamp, a toggle clamp or a positive lock), because eccentric cams can shake loose and have only one true locking point (Carr Lane). Avoid it where a sideways or twisting load tries to drag the clamped parts, such as disc-brake wheels, where a through-axle is safer (Wikipedia, Sheldon Brown). Don't use it where the clamp must hold a precise position against large loads on its own; add a pin or shoulder to locate the parts. If you need even more holding force or a firm over-centre lock with a visible open/closed state, use a toggle clamp or draw latch; for a permanent joint, use a bolt or glue.
- **environment_limits**: BikeRadar suggests testing a skewer with a drop of oil on the cam to reveal poor designs). Vibration can loosen eccentric and spiral cams (Carr Lane). Temperature swings make pole materials swell or shrink slightly, which can make cam-locked pole sections slip (MSR). Printed plastic cams and levers creep under constant load and lose clamping force over time (Hackaday). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: A bike quick release that is left open, or closed like a wing nut without real clamping force, can let the wheel fall out; forks have 'lawyer lips' (raised tabs) as a backup, and disc-brake forces tend to push the axle out of the dropouts (Wikipedia, Sheldon Brown). On external-cam skewers the curved plastic washer can rotate so the cam presses on its flat and the clamp goes loose enough for the wheel to fall out (Sheldon Brown). Quick-release wheels are easy to steal (Wikipedia). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: Not actually clamped: the lever is closed without enough resistance, or used like a wing nut (Sheldon Brown); fix by tightening the adjusting nut until closing leaves a mark on your palm. Adjusting nut too tight, so the lever can't close fully and sits short of its locking point (Sheldon Brown). Brainy Biker). Slipping when contact pads glaze or pivot holes wear oval, and crushed cam lobes in softwood clamps (Firgelli). Shaking loose under vibration (Carr Lane). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Toggle clamp and over-centre linkage: use links that go past a straight line instead of a cam; usually give more holding force and a more definite locked position
  - Draw latch: an over-centre lever that pulls two parts together through a hook, for lids and cases
  - Eccentric adjuster (eccentric nut or bushing): uses the same off-centre idea to make small position adjustments, not to clamp and release quickly
  - Spiral cam clamp: a cam with a spiral (involute) face that can lock anywhere in its throw rather than at one point (Carr Lane)
  - Wing nut or thumb screw: tool-free too, but slower; holds by screw friction and is less sensitive to how it is set
  - Bicycle through-axle: a threaded axle that replaced skewers on many disc-brake bikes because it is not pushed out by braking forces (Wikipedia)
  - Cam buckle and cam cleat: use a toothed cam to grip a strap or rope rather than to clamp two rigid parts
  - Furniture cam lock (cam fitting): a different part with a similar name, a rotating disc that pulls a bolt to join flat-pack panels

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [Quick release scewer.JPG (Wikimedia Commons)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Quick_release_scewer.JPG): Photo of a bicycle quick-release skewer with its cam lever, rod and nut *(licence: CC BY-SA 3.0 or GFDL 1.2+ (AndrewDressel))*
  - [Bicycle Quick-Release Mechanisms (Sheldon Brown)](https://www.sheldonbrown.com/skewers.html): Photos of open, adjusted and closed quick releases, internal versus external cams, lawyer-lip dropouts and a hazard diagram of the external-cam washer *(licence: link only)*
  - [Clamping lever with eccentrical cam GN 927 (JW Winco, YouTube)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vaeRNEE9wmU): Manufacturer video of an industrial eccentric cam clamping lever (title and channel confirmed via YouTube oEmbed; content not viewed) *(licence: link only)*
  - [THIS Print Will Keep Your Bag of Chips Closed (Chip Clip!) (3D Printing Nerd, YouTube)](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fkTCNT-1sJ8): Review of Andrei's print-in-place cam bag clip, a 3D-printed eccentric cam lever (title and channel confirmed via YouTube oEmbed; linked from the model page) *(licence: link only)*
  - [Different Types of Cam Clamps: The Comprehensive Guide (Carr Lane)](https://www.carrlane.com/engineering-resources/technical-information/manual-workholding/cam-clamps): Illustrated guide to eccentric versus spiral cams and cam-action strap, edge, up-thrust and hold-down clamps *(licence: link only)*

Details read through the Printables public API because the web page blocks automated access *(licence: GPL 3.0)*

Read through the Printables public API *(licence: CC BY-NC)* *(unverified parts omitted)*

## 23 Bistable compliant mechanism (snap-through switch or hinge)

*compliant mechanisms & flexures.* A one-piece springy part with two stable positions that snaps from one to the other, like a hair clip or a snap bracelet

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A one-piece springy part that happily rests in either of two positions and snaps from one to the other when pushed past a middle point, like a snap hair clip, a snap bracelet or a light switch that clicks on and off
- **term**: Bistable compliant mechanism (snap-through mechanism)
- **aliases**: 
  - bistable mechanism
  - snap-through mechanism or snap-through switch
  - snap-action flexure or snap-action mechanism
  - bistable switch
  - bistable hinge, snap hinge or butterfly hinge (flip-top bottle caps)
  - bistable clip or clasp
  - curved-beam bistable mechanism or double curved beam (engineering literature)
  - buckled-beam bistable mechanism
  - von Mises truss (the classic two-bar snap-through model)
  - tape spring or bistable strip (snap bracelet, tape measure style)
  - snap dome or click dome (dome-shaped version)
  - multistable mechanism (versions with more than two stable positions)
  - hair clip mechanism (HCM, robotics research term)
  - slap bracelet or snap bracelet (everyday name for the strip version)
- **family**: compliant mechanisms & flexures
- **other_families**: over-centre, toggle & cam clamps, springs & energy storage, indexing & detents, hinges & pivots, snap & clip fits, print-in-place & 3D-printing joints
- **one_liner**: A part that bends instead of using hinges and has two resting shapes, so pushing it past a tipping point makes it snap quickly into the other shape and stay there with no latch, spring or power holding it.
- **complexity**: Single piece in its simplest form (a pre-curved beam, a pair of beams joined at the middle, a curved strip or a dome, or links joined by thin flexible hinges). Some designs add a separate spring, magnets, pins or a housing, but the snapping comes from the flexing parts themselves.
- **how_it_works**: Bending the part stores energy in it like a spring. The shape is chosen so the stored energy first rises as you push it, peaks at a middle (unstable) point, and then falls again to a second low point, so after the peak the part pushes itself the rest of the way and snaps into its second shape, where it rests with no force needed (Chi et al. 2022, 'ball on a hill' picture from BYU CMR). For a pre-curved beam this works only if the beam's curve is tall enough compared with its thickness and the beam is stopped from twisting sideways as it flips, which is why designs often use two beams joined at the middle (Qiu, Lang and Slocum 2004). Pushing back past the tipping point snaps it back, so it acts like a switch with two positions.

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - click into set positions: two (or more) positions that it snaps into and holds
  - store energy: holds strain energy and releases it quickly in the snap
  - let it move (turn, slide or bend): moves by bending, with no pins or sliding surfaces
  - join and hold: bistable clips and clasps hold cables, hair or lids closed
  - lock, and release by hand: a clip or lid stays shut or open until pushed past its tipping point
- **motion**: flex: the part bends to move a point along a line (snap-through beams and switches) or to swing a lid or arm (bistable hinges, e.g. up to 180 degrees on BYU's bistable-to-monostable hinge); the snap itself is fast, taking only several to tens of milliseconds (Chi et al. 2022)
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: it moves along one path between its two stable shapes; the flexures are designed to be stiff in the other directions
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - over-centre (snaps past a point and stays): once pushed past the unstable middle point, it snaps to the second position and stays there with no latch (Chi et al. 2022)
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): two stable positions, or more in multistable designs made by chaining bistable units (Chi et al. 2022)
  - quick-release: bistable clips and cable clips open and close with a push of the fingers (Cableclips, Printables)
- **holding_principle**: 
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): each stable position is a low point in the stored spring energy of the flexing part itself
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): clips and hinges also use their closed shape to wrap around or cover what they hold
- **input_output**: not applicable as a transmission. It acts as a threshold or trigger: a small push that exceeds the snap-through force -> a fast, larger jump to the other position, which can give an amplified output force during the snap (Chi et al. 2022). Not back-drivable in the usual sense: a load smaller than the snap-through force leaves it where it is; a larger one flips it.
- **power**: passive (no power needed). When combined with an actuator (motor, magnet, shape-memory wire, air pressure) power is needed only to switch it, because it can apply a contact force without continued power (Qiu et al. 2004); if power is lost it keeps its last state
- **removability**: tool-free quick release: as a clip or clasp it opens and closes by hand; as a switch or hinge it is built into the part itself (one piece), so there is nothing to take apart
- **reuse_cycles**: many times with a tough, springy material (polypropylene, PETG, nylon, spring steel); only a few tens of cycles with brittle PLA flexures, e.g. about 40 to 50 cycles for early all-flexure versions of one printed switch (Gamma4D, Printables) and about 25 cycles for a single-strand FDM living hinge (Hubs)
- **tools_needed**: none
- **seal**: none
- **load**: light: it holds only against forces smaller than its snap-through force, and printed versions are small plastic flexures. Strongest along its snapping direction up to that force; weak against sideways loads, which can twist the flexing beams so they no longer snap cleanly (Qiu et al. 2004)
- **adjustable**: no: the snap force and the two positions are set by the geometry (beam thickness, curve height, hinge angle, material); changing them means reprinting. Some research designs tune or switch off bistability with magnets or added parts (BYU bistable-to-monostable magnetic hinge; Chi et al. 2022)
- **play_and_precision**: Play: none, because there are no pin joints; compliant mechanisms reduce or remove backlash (BYU CMR, Printables). Misalignment: off-axis pushes can twist the beams instead of snapping them (Qiu et al. 2004) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: not applicable
- **materials**: Polypropylene is the best choice for moulded or milled flexing hinges (revpart; BYU recommends milled polypropylene sheet for its bistable switch). For FDM printing: PETG, ASA or ABS (Cableclips; PLA 'is not elastic enough'), nylon (Hubs), TPU for flexible dome and auxetic versions (PLA 'too brittle', Printables 604690). Metal versions use thin spring or stainless steel (slap bracelets use steel bands about 0.15 mm thick; Wikipedia) and robotics 'hair clip' mechanisms use PETG sheet about 0.38 to 0.76 mm thick, carbon fibre or steel (arXiv 2311.03212). Silicon for microscopic (MEMS) versions (Qiu et al. 2004)
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: it prints in one piece with no assembly, but getting a crisp snap that lasts needs the right material, print orientation and beam thickness, usually found by printing a few test versions

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): one-piece switches, clips, hinges and fidget toys
  - CNC: milled polypropylene sheet (BYU recommends this for its bistable switch)
  - sheet metal: snap domes and bistable steel strips (snap bracelets) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: yes: most designs print as one working piece (BYU bistable mechanism and switch, Cableclips); a few use short pieces of filament as pins, e.g. Gamma4D's hinged switch
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Choose a tough, springy plastic: Cableclips are tested in PETG, ASA and ABS, and PLA is not recommended because it is not elastic enough (Printables 328519); PLA flexures can wear out in tens of cycles, whereas a pinned-hinge version of the same switch passed 110,000 cycles in PLA (Gamma4D, Printables 488317). BYU notes that the fatigue and flex properties of your filament decide how many cycles a printed bistable switch lasts (Printables 581016). Orient the part so the flexing beams lie flat on the bed with layers running along their length, so bending does not pull layers apart; Hubs advises building the hinge so its width, not its length, is stacked layer by layer, and making FDM flexures 0.4 to 0.6 mm thick with at least two layers (Hubs). Print orientation of the springy parts is critical and a wrong orientation won't work (Hackaday, snap action mechanism). Use the slicer settings the designer gives, because wall count and line width set the flexure thickness (Cableclips). Give pre-curved snap beams a curve height well over 2.3 times their thickness and stop them twisting, e.g. with two beams joined at the middle (see rules of thumb). Round the corners where flexures meet thick parts, since flexure roots concentrate stress and fail by fatigue (Wikipedia, Compliant mechanism). Hubs suggests annealing living hinges in boiling water and flexing them while warm to extend life (Hubs). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Mostly made, not bought. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Pre-curved beam: with Q = curve (apex) height / thickness, Q below 1.23 bends smoothly, 1.23 to 2.31 snaps but springs back (monostable), and above 2.31 it is bistable (Chi et al. 2022, Advanced Materials, citing Qiu et al.)
  - A single curved beam also needs its twisting (second) buckling mode blocked, e.g. by guiding the centre in a slot or, better, by joining two parallel curved beams at their centres; a gap of five beam thicknesses between the beams worked (Qiu, Lang and Slocum 2004)
  - Linkage with flexible hinges (von Mises truss): it becomes bistable when the links' incline angle is larger than a critical angle arccos(L1/L) (Chi et al. 2022)
  - Link-and-spring version: bistable when the rigid link length is greater than the spring's initial length (Chi et al. 2022)
  - Curved strips and plates (snap bracelet, tape measure): bistable when width x square root of (curvature / thickness) is much greater than 1, i.e. wide, thin, strongly curved strips (Chi et al. 2022)
  - FDM living hinges 0.4 to 0.6 mm thick with at least two layers; SLS 0.3 to 0.8 mm; FDM single-strand hinges last about 25 cycles and SLS 30 to 50 before failure (Hubs)
  - Moulded polypropylene living hinges 0.007 to 0.015 in (about 0.18 to 0.38 mm) thick, in homopolymer PP, flexed a few times straight out of the mould; butterfly and three-section bistable hinges give the flip action of dispensing caps (revpart)

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Snap hair clips (Chi et al. 2022)
  - Snap (slap) bracelets, made of layered bistable steel bands (Wikipedia, Slap bracelet)
  - Flip-top shampoo and ketchup bottle caps with snap hinges (Chi et al. 2022; US patent 4982866)
  - Light switches and popper jumping toys (BYU CMR; Chi et al. 2022)
- **when_to_use**: Use a bistable compliant mechanism when you want something to stay in one of two positions without a latch or power: an on/off switch or toggle, a clip or clasp that snaps open and shut, a lid or hinge that stays open or closed, a gripper that snaps shut on contact, or a valve or relay that only uses power to change state. It is ideal for 3D printing because it can be one piece with no assembly, no backlash and nothing to lubricate (BYU CMR), and its fast snap gives a clear click and a burst of force (Chi et al. 2022).
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it for heavy loads or where it must hold against forces larger than its snap force (use a latch or an over-centre toggle clamp). Avoid printed plastic flexures for parts that must switch many thousands of times in PLA (use a pinned spring toggle, a metal spring or PP/nylon) and for parts held in the bent state in heat for long periods, where creep weakens the snap. Where you need many set positions, use a detent or ratchet; where you need a firm adjustable clamp, use a cam lever or toggle clamp; for a push-once-to-latch, push-again-to-release action, use a push-push latch.
- **environment_limits**: Plastic flexures fatigue at their stress concentrations under repeated flexing (Wikipedia, Compliant mechanism) and plastic springs slowly deform (creep) under steady strain (Hackaday); PLA is the worst common filament for this (Hackaday; Cableclips). Energy is partly lost in each flex, which adds damping (Wikipedia, Compliant mechanism). Steel versions resist creep but can rust or crack through the covering (Wikipedia, Slap bracelet). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Metal strip versions can break or poke through their covering and cut, which led to school bans and recalls of cheap slap bracelets (Wikipedia, Slap bracelet). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: Won't stay in the second position (it snaps and springs back), because the curve is too shallow for its thickness or the beam twists instead of snapping; fix by increasing curve height over thickness or joining two beams at the middle (Qiu et al. 2004; Chi et al. 2022). Cracks at the roots of flexures from fatigue, especially in PLA (Wikipedia, Compliant mechanism; Gamma4D; Hubs). Layers splitting when the part was printed in the wrong orientation (Hackaday snap action mechanism). Snap getting weak or 'sloppy' with heavy use or after long storage in one state, from wear and creep (Hackaday; *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **related**: 
  - Over-centre linkage and spring toggle: the rigid-link version with pin joints and a separate spring; more durable in PLA but more parts
  - Living hinge: a single thin flexing hinge with no stable positions of its own; add a snap geometry to make it bistable
  - Push-push latch: also has two states, but switches with the same push each time using a heart-shaped track
  - Click-pen mechanism: a rotating-cam indexer that alternates between two states on each push
  - Cantilever snap fit: a springy hook that snaps into a catch to join parts, rather than switching a part between two resting shapes
  - Tape-spring hinge: a curved metal strip that folds flat and snaps back straight, used for deployable structures
  - Ball detent: a spring-loaded ball that clicks into several set positions
  - Constant-force and negative-stiffness mechanisms: use the same buckling physics to flatten or reverse a spring's force curve

### Pictures, videos and models

- **links**: 
  - [Bistable compliant switch mechanism.png (Wikimedia Commons)](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Bistable_compliant_switch_mechanism.png): Image of BYU's one-piece bistable compliant switch, used on the Wikipedia article Compliant mechanism *(licence: CC BY-SA 4.0 (BYU CMR))*
  - [Bistable Compliant Mechanism (BYU CMR, Printables)](https://www.printables.com/model/581013-bistable-compliant-mechanism): Animated GIF of a one-piece printed bistable mechanism snapping between its two positions, with references to BYU papers *(licence: CC BY-NC-SA (model page read via the Printables public API))*
  - [Simple Snap Action Mechanism Is 100% 3D Printed (Hackaday)](https://hackaday.com/2022/06/10/simple-snap-action-mechanism-is-100-3d-printed/): Article and pictures of a fully printed snap-action slide with two curved leaf springs, stressing print orientation *(licence: link only)*
  - [Bistable and Multistable Actuators for Soft Robots: Structures, Materials, and Functionalities (Chi et al., Advanced Materials 2022)](https://d-nb.info/1254133046/34): Open-access review with diagrams of curved beams, curved plates, domes and flexure linkages, force-displacement and energy curves, and photos of everyday bistable objects (hair clips, tape measures, bottle caps) *(licence: Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY; open access))* *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **make_it_yourself**: 
  - [Bistable Compliant Switch (BYU CMR, Printables)](https://www.printables.com/model/581016-bistable-compliant-switch): One-piece bistable switch based on a four-bar linkage with living hinges, with a short lesson on bistability; print in PLA or ABS or mill from 1/4 inch polypropylene sheet. Read through the Printables public API *(licence: CC BY-NC)*
  - [Cableclips - Bistable Compliant Mechanism (Bodo, Printables)](https://www.printables.com/model/328519-cableclips-bistable-compliant-mechanism): Reusable snap-open cable clips in nine sizes (8.5 to 30 mm); tested in PETG, ASA and ABS; PLA not recommended. Read through the Printables public API *(licence: CC BY-NC-ND)*

Read through the Printables public API *(licence: CC BY)*

## 24 Push-push latch (push-to-open, heart-cam latch)

*locks, latches & catches.* Push once to latch, push again to release; a pin follows a heart-shaped track that holds it on alternate pushes (cabinet doors, SD card slots)

### What it is

- **plain_name**: A hidden catch that locks when you push a door, drawer or card in, and lets go and pops it out when you push it again, like a handle-less kitchen cupboard or a phone's memory-card slot
- **term**: Push-push latch (heart-cam latch)
- **aliases**: 
  - push-to-open latch
  - push latch
  - touch latch (also used for the magnetic type)
  - push-push mechanism or push-on/push-off mechanism
  - heart cam or heart-shaped cam groove
  - cardioid (heart-curve) latch
  - push lock mechanism or push-lock switch (latching push-button switches)
  - push-push card connector or push-push eject (SD and microSD card sockets)
  - cam groove latch
  - push-push locking bolt or indexing plunger with push-push lock (industrial standard part, e.g. GN 514)
  - ballpoint-pen principle (German 'Kugelschreiberprinzip')
  - Herzkurve (German: heart curve)
  - Tip-On (brand) for piston-type push-to-open furniture fittings (Deslaurier)
- **family**: locks, latches & catches
- **other_families**: indexing & detents, quick-release & tool-free
- **one_liner**: A spring-loaded catch in which a small pin runs round a closed, heart-shaped track, so the first push parks the pin in the notch of the heart and holds the part in, and the next push frees it so the spring pushes the part back out.
- **complexity**: A small assembly that acts as one unit: a sliding part (plunger, slider or card tray) with a heart-shaped groove or cam, a follower pin on a pivoting arm or springy wire that can swing sideways, a return spring that pushes the slider outward, and a housing; cabinet versions add a strike on the door. In some designs the heart is on the fixed part and the pin moves with the slider.
- **how_it_works**: A spring always pushes the slider outward, and a pin that can swing sideways runs in a closed loop groove shaped like a heart. When you push in past the closed position (overtravel), the pin runs up one side of the heart; when you let go, the angled wall steers it into the notch at the top of the heart, where the spring holds it trapped, so the part stays in (latched). Push again and the pin leaves the notch towards the other side; let go and the spring carries it down the other side of the heart back to the start, pushing the part out (Kapy CAD; Thang, 'Push-push button 2'; DaimlerChrysler/Fischer patent EP1253271A2). Small steps in the groove floor, with the pin pressed into the groove, or offset corners in the track make sure the pin can only go round one way (Kapy CAD; Omron patent US20150107980A1).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - lock, and release by hand: holds a door, drawer, lid, card or button in, and releases it with a second push
  - click into set positions: alternates between two positions (in and latched, out and released) on each push
  - join and hold: keeps handle-less doors and drawers closed
- **motion**: slide: a short straight in-and-out stroke of the slider (with a small overtravel past the latched position), while the follower pin swings slightly sideways round the heart
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1: the slider moves along one straight line; the pin's sideways swing is guided by the track and is not a free motion
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): two alternating states; every two pushes send the pin once round the heart
  - quick-release: a push opens it and the spring pops the door, drawer or card out, so no handle or tool is needed
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the pin is trapped in the notch of the heart
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): the return spring holds the pin in the notch and pops the part out when released
- **input_output**: Repeated straight pushes -> alternating latched and released states (a push-push toggle); one lap of the heart track per two pushes. In-line: the push and the spring act along the same line. Back-drivable: no; pulling on the latched part does not release it, and pulling hard can break the cam surface or bend the pin (Alps patent US4916276A). A hard shock that pushes the part further in, such as a car crash, can accidentally release it (EP1253271A2).
- **power**: passive (no power needed)
- **removability**: tool-free quick release: the latched part is released with a push; the latch body itself is screwed, pressed or clipped into the cabinet or device
- **reuse_cycles**: many times: designed for everyday use; e.g. push-push SD card connectors rated for more than 10,000 cycles, or 5,000 for an ultra-low-profile microSD version (MoarConn); printed versions wear out sooner as the steps round off
- **seal**: none
- **load**: light: holds a door, drawer, card or button in against small forces only; magnetic push-to-open cabinet latches are rated about 2 to 6 lbf (9 to 27 N) pull (McMaster-Carr); industrial push-push locking bolts cannot take axial load, and their pin retracts only by spring force (Konstruktionspraxis on GN 514). Weak against pulling the latched part out (Alps patent)
- **play_and_precision**: Noticeable: the latched position sits a little short of the fully pushed position, and the part needs room to be pushed further in (overtravel) to latch or release (Kapy CAD; EP1253271A2). The pin-to-groove fit must be free but not loose, like a fine sliding fit: too tight and it seizes, too loose and the pin skips or derails (Kapy CAD). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **access_needed**: one side only (blind): you just push on the front of the door, drawer or card; the latch is hidden inside
- **materials**: Moulded plastic housings and sliders with steel return springs; metal eject levers carrying the heart groove in card connectors (JAE patent US20020192991A1); polyamide (PA) button and nitrided steel pin on the GN 514 push-push locking bolt (Konstruktionspraxis). Printed versions: PETG or PLA bodies, a metal pen spring or compression spring, and ideally a steel wire (e.g. bent paperclip) as the follower arm (Printables 1601839, 496329)
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: easy to buy or to print from a proven model, but designing your own needs a crisp track, careful clearances, a metal spring and preferably a metal follower pin, because printed arms and springs creep and wear

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - off-the-shelf part (push-to-open cabinet latches, push-push card connectors, latching push-button switches, push-push locking bolts)
  - 3D print (FDM): body, slider and track, with a metal spring and wire follower *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: partly: the body and track print well but most reliable designs add a metal compression or pen spring and often a steel wire follower (Printables 496329, 1601839); fully printed versions with printed springs exist but their springs and arms creep when left latched for days (Printables 534635, 376606)
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Split the part into a body and a cap that carries the track, so the heart groove is an open channel on a flat face with no internal overhangs (Kapy CAD). The steps in the groove floor that force one-way travel are the mechanism, not decoration: make them several full layers tall, about 0.5 to 1.5 mm, with the blocking face printed vertical so it stays sharp (Kapy CAD). Use a real metal spring with enough force across the whole stroke; printed springs relax and give inconsistent latching (Kapy CAD); one fully printed latch worked many times but its printed springs deformed permanently after being held compressed for several days (Printables 534635). A printed follower arm left bent in the latched position deformed until the case stopped opening, so the designer changed the track so the arm is not bent when closed (Printables 376606, which also recommends PETG for the arm and spring); a bent steel paperclip as the follower is more durable, more precise and allows a smaller mechanism (Printables 1601839, 12 mm x 10 mm with a pen spring). Aim for a free but not loose fit between pin and groove, measure the printed groove, and deepen the track if the pin derails (Kapy CAD). A 0.4 mm nozzle rounds the heart's sharp corners; consider a finer nozzle for small tracks (Kapy CAD). Proven printable examples use PETG with a standard ballpoint pen spring (Printables 496329) or PLA with two small compression springs in a unit about 30 mm x 30 mm (Printables 273742).
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search: 'push to open latch', 'push latch', 'touch latch' (mechanical or magnetic), 'push-to-open magnetic latch' (McMaster-Carr), 'push-open drawer fitting', 'push-push card connector' or 'push-push microSD socket' (Hirose, Molex, Amphenol and others), 'push lock switch' or 'latching push button switch', 'indexing plunger with push-push lock' (e.g. GN 514, Ganter/JW Winco), 'heart cam'. Also 'ballpoint pen spring' for printed builds.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - Groove-floor steps several layers tall, about 0.5 to 1.5 mm, with the blocking face vertical; a metal coil spring, not a printed flexure; pin-to-groove clearance like a fine sliding fit (Kapy CAD, 'Push-push latching button with a heart cam')
  - Steps in the groove floor wear with repeated use and let the pin go the wrong way; offsetting the track's corners in the direction of travel restricts direction even when worn (Omron patent US20150107980A1)
  - Don't let a pull on the latched part load the pin and heart directly: Alps separated the driving member from the slider so pulling does not break the cam or bend the pin (US4916276A)
  - Add an inertia lock where a crash or drop could push the part further in and release it, as in car glove boxes and drawers (DaimlerChrysler/Fischer, EP1253271A2)
  - A reliable printed single-spring push latch can be as small as 12 mm x 10 mm using a pen spring and a paperclip follower; making the walls thinner caused failures under spring load (Justin Scroggs, Printables 1601839)
  - Push-push card connectors are rated for thousands of cycles (over 10,000; 5,000 for an ultra-low-profile microSD) and may need a secondary lock in vibrating equipment (MoarConn)

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - Handle-less kitchen cabinets and drawers that open with a push (Deslaurier; McMaster-Carr)
  - SD and microSD card slots that click the card in and push it out again (JAE patent; MoarConn)
  - Car glove boxes, drawers and storage compartments (EP1253271A2)
  - Latching push-button switches that stay in until pushed again (Alps patent; Thang, 'Push-push switch')
- **when_to_use**: Use a push-push latch when you want a clean front with no handle or knob, when the user has only one hand or a fingertip free, or when a small item such as a card, battery tray or lid should click in and pop out: handle-less cabinets and drawers, hidden compartments, printed boxes and cases, card and cartridge slots, and on/off push buttons. It is also a neat way to make a single button toggle between two states.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it where the latched part is pulled or loaded outward, because the pin and heart track are small and can break (Alps patent), and where shocks or vibration could press the part in and release it (EP1253271A2; MoarConn), unless you add a second lock. Avoid it where children or accidental bumps should not open things, since mechanical push-to-open fittings open with a light push (Deslaurier); use a child-resistant latch or a magnetic catch that needs more force. For heavy doors, sink cabinets with little space behind the door, or big corner cabinets, Deslaurier advises against push-to-open latches; use a handle with a ball catch, roller catch or magnetic catch. If you only need a panel to stay shut, a magnetic catch or ball catch is simpler.
- **environment_limits**: Printed plastic springs and follower arms creep when the latch is left closed for days, so the latch stops popping out fully (Printables 534635, 376606); plastic springs always deform permanently under steady load and PLA is a very poor spring material (Hackaday). The groove steps wear with use (Omron patent). Vibration and shocks can unlatch it (MoarConn; EP1253271A2). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: The door or drawer pops open by spring force when bumped, which can surprise people or let small children open cupboards; lighter fittings open more easily by accident (Deslaurier). In vehicles, a crash can push a latched drawer or glove box in far enough to release it and throw its contents into the cabin (EP1253271A2). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **common_failures**: Presses but doesn't latch because the floor steps are too low or rounded, or latches only sometimes because the spring is weak or relaxed (Kapy CAD). Seizes mid-cycle when the groove is too tight; skips or derails when too loose or too shallow (Kapy CAD). Stops latching after much use as the steps wear and the pin goes the wrong way (Omron patent). Cam surface broken or pin bent by someone pulling the latched part (Alps patent). Printed follower arms and springs deforming after being held latched (Printables 376606, 534635). Unwanted release from vibration or shock (MoarConn; EP1253271A2).
- **related**: 
  - Click-pen mechanism (rotating-cam push-push indexer): does the same push-on, push-off job with a toothed cam that turns one step per push instead of a pin running round a heart
  - Magnetic touch latch: a spring plunger with a magnet; holds a little more firmly and needs more force to open, but can't be used on drawers (Deslaurier)
  - Ball catch or roller catch: a spring-loaded ball or roller that simply holds a door shut; needs a handle to pull it open
  - Bistable compliant mechanism: a one-piece springy part with two resting positions, switched by pushing back and forth rather than pushing the same way twice
  - Push-to-close cam latch: latches automatically when a door is pushed shut, but opens with a handle or button rather than a second push (Southco)
  - Compliant one-piece latch: a printed springy latch released by bending a handle
  - Push-push locking bolt (indexing plunger): an industrial pin that extends and retracts with alternate pushes using a heart curve (GN 514)

Read through the Printables public API *(licence: CC BY-NC-SA)*

  - https://www.printables.com/model/534635-fully-3d-printable-push-latch-mechanism-wip
  - https://api.printables.com/graphql/

## 25 Click-pen mechanism (rotating-cam push-push indexer)

*indexing & detents.* The retractable-pen mechanism, where each push turns a toothed cam one step so it alternately holds the tip out and lets it back in

### What it is

- **plain_name**: The clicky button on a retractable pen: push once and the tip pops out and stays out, push again and it goes back in
- **term**: Rotating-cam push-push mechanism (click-pen mechanism, rotary ratchet cam indexer)
- **aliases**: 
  - click pen mechanism / clicky pen mechanism
  - retractable pen mechanism / retractable ballpoint mechanism
  - protract-retract mechanism (patent wording, US 3,137,276)
  - rotating-ratcheting mechanism (patent wording for flashlight clicker switches, US 4,827,385)
  - clicker switch / clicky tail switch (flashlights)
  - alternate-action mechanism / push-on push-off (latching push-button switches)
  - push-push switch or push-push button (Nguyen Duc Thang's animation names)
  - barrel cam for 180 deg. rotation (Thang's name for the cylindrical-cam version)
  - thrust device, cam body, rotator, plunger (names for its parts)
  - rotary cam indexer / push-to-index (when each push is used to step a dial round)
- **family**: indexing & detents
- **other_families**: locks, latches & catches, motion conversion, one-way & ratchet
- **one_liner**: A push button, a spring and a small toothed part that turns a fixed step every time you push, so the same push alternately locks a part out and lets it spring back in (or steps a dial round by one position).
- **complexity**: A small assembly that acts as one unit, usually 3 moving parts plus a spring: (1) a push button (plunger) with sloped saw teeth on its lower end; (2) a turning part (the rotator, cam body or cam follower) with lugs or teeth, sitting on the refill or shaft; (3) a fixed tube (the barrel or housing) whose inside wall has ribs separated by channels of two kinds, long deep channels and short shallow notches; (4) a return spring that pushes the rotator back up. The Sketchfab cutaway by C. Yamahata names the three main parts as push button (with ratchet), follower (spring-loaded by the ink cartridge) and cam.
- **how_it_works**: When you push the button, its sloped teeth drive the rotator down past the ends of the fixed ribs inside the tube. Pressed against those slopes by the spring, the rotator twists a little. When you let go, the spring pushes it back up and it slides along the sloped top of the nearest rib, twisting a little more, until its lugs drop into the next gap. The gaps alternate: a shallow notch catches the lugs and holds the tip out, while a deep channel lets the lugs slide all the way up so the spring pulls the tip back in (US 3,137,276; Thang, 'Barrel cam for 180 deg. rotation': 'Key factor is different depths of the cam groove'). Because the rotator always turns the same way by one step per push, every push swaps between the two states, and the clicks you hear are the rotator snapping onto the ledges (Pen Vibe; Make:).

### Finder attributes

- **job**: 
  - click into set positions: each push steps the rotator round one position and it stops there
  - lock, and release by hand: the first push locks the tip out against writing pressure, the next push releases it
  - change the kind of motion: a straight push becomes a stepped turn (used to rotate a dial or switch contacts)
- **motion**: rotate+slide: the button slides a few millimetres; the rotator slides in and out and turns one step (a fraction of a turn) per push, always in the same direction
- **degrees_of_freedom**: 1 as the user sees it: the button only slides in and out. Inside, the rotator both slides and turns, but the cam teeth tie the two together, so the push alone decides where it goes
- **special_behaviour**: 
  - indexing (clicks into set positions): the rotator steps round one position per push and rests on a ledge or in a channel
  - one-way (moves freely one way, locks the other): the sloped teeth only ever turn the rotator in one direction, like a ratchet
  - hand-release only (locks under load, releases when a person operates it): once the tip is out, writing pressure pushes the lugs harder onto the ledges, so only another push on the button retracts it
  - motion conversion (e.g. rotary to linear): a straight push becomes a stepped rotation; flashlight clicker switches, the Hackaday 'signspinner' indicator and Thang's 'Push-push switch' use that rotation to turn contacts or a dial
- **holding_principle**: 
  - shape (the parts interlock; form-fit): the rotator's lugs sit on the ledges (shallow notches) of the fixed ribs
  - spring force (a spring or springy part presses it in place): the return spring keeps the lugs pressed onto the ledges or up the channels
- **input_output**: Linear push (a stroke of a few mm) -> alternating two-position output along the same axis (tip out / tip in), plus a stepped rotation of the rotator. One full out-and-in cycle takes two pushes. Shafts in line. Efficiency is low because every click slides plastic over plastic against the spring. Back-drivable: no; pushing on the tip or pulling the button does not change state, only another full push does. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **power**: passive (no power needed); electrical switches built on it (flashlight clicky switches, latching push buttons) use it to open and close contacts but do not need power to move it
- **removability**: removable: pens and printed demos unscrew or pull apart so the refill and spring can be replaced; the mechanism itself is not a joining method
- **tools_needed**: none to use; to build a printed one you need the printed parts, a spring (a spare ballpoint-pen spring is common) and sometimes grease or fine sandpaper (Printables models 695673 and 114490)
- **seal**: none: nothing in it seals, and dirt or lint in the teeth can make it stick
- **load**: Light. It only has to hold the tip against writing pressure, which pushes the lugs straight onto the ledges (strong direction). *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **adjustable**: no: the number of positions and the stroke are fixed by the tooth and channel layout; to change them you redesign or reprint
- **play_and_precision**: Small axial play: the lugs come to rest in the same place each time because they sit on fixed ledges. Rotation is repeatable to one step. It needs the parts to be concentric; if the button or rotator wobbles in the tube, the teeth can miss and it jams or skips.
- **access_needed**: end of a tube or shaft: the parts slide into the end of a tube (a pen barrel or switch housing) and the button sticks out of that end
- **materials**: Moulded plastic in pens and switches: US 3,137,276 notes that the push button and rotating lug member 'can also be constituted by injection molded pieces'. Printed demos work in PLA with a steel pen spring (Printables 695673, 127961); a printed spring needs PETG or ABS rather than PLA (Hackaday signspinner). It is a mechanism, not a way of joining materials. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **maker_difficulty**: moderate: the parts are small and the sloped teeth must meet cleanly, so pen-sized versions are hard to print well; makers print it larger (120-150% scale on one Printables model), grease or sand the sliding faces, and expect the first clicks to 'grind in'

### Making it

- **processes**: 
  - 3D print (FDM): works best when printed larger than pen size; several free demo and complete-pen models exist
  - 3D print (powder bed, SLS or MJF nylon): one designer made a complete working click pen as a single printed piece that only powder-bed printers can make (Printables 1191787)
  - injection moulding: how commercial pens and clicker switches are made (US 3,137,276)
  - off-the-shelf part: a spare retractable pen, its refill and spring, or a latching push-button switch already contain the mechanism *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **print_in_place**: no for FDM: the button, rotator and tube are printed separately and slid together with a metal spring (or a printed PETG spring or a rubber band). The exception is a single-piece click pen designed for SLS or MJF powder-bed printing, which is fully working straight off the printer apart from the refill (Printables 1191787).
- **making_tips_3d_printing**: Size: pen-sized teeth are at the edge of what FDM can print; one designer recommends printing their mechanism at 120-150% scale and says their smallest version 'is difficult to print easily' (Printables 114490). Orientation: print the tube and rotator standing up so the teeth and ribs are round and centred, and print without supports where the model allows (all the Printables demos opened print without supports); keep slopes at or below about 45-60 degrees from vertical so they print cleanly (Prusa). Layers: 0.15-0.2 mm layers give smoother slopes (Printables 695673 uses 0.15 mm, 2 perimeters, 20% infill, PLA; the rorys3D 'CLICK pen' uses 0.2 mm and PLA). Clearance: leave about 0.3 mm between parts that slide or turn (Prusa); too tight and the rotator will not twist, too loose and the lugs miss the ledges. Finishing: remove any brim, clean the tips of the plunger teeth, and grease or lightly sand the sloped faces; the first few clicks grind the parts in (Printables 695673, 127961, 1051321). Spring: use a steel pen spring (Printables 695673 uses a 4 mm x 20 mm spring from a ballpoint pen) because plastic springs held compressed creep and weaken (DuPont design guide, pp. 36-37); if you must print the spring, use PETG or ABS, not PLA (Hackaday signspinner; Hackaday 2026 on printed springs). Typical failures: the rotator sticks half-way (burrs, elephant's foot or too little clearance), it skips straight through without locking (rounded ledge tips, weak spring, wrong scale), or it wears after many clicks (use PETG or nylon for the rotator and grease it).
- **off_the_shelf_parts**: Search terms: 'retractable pen refill' and 'pen spring' (to reuse in printed pens); 'latching push button switch', 'alternate action push button switch' or 'push-on push-off switch' (switches with the mechanism built in); 'clicky tail switch' or 'forward clicky' / 'reverse clicky' switch (flashlight parts). A cheap retractable pen is the easiest source of a working mechanism to study.
- **rules_of_thumb**: 
  - The fixed ribs inside the tube are separated by two kinds of gap: deep channels that let the rotator's lugs slide right up (tip in) and shallow ones that stop them (tip out). Deep and shallow gaps alternate round the tube (US 3,137,276; Thang, 'Barrel cam for 180 deg. rotation 1b/2b': 'Key factor is different depths of the cam groove').
  - One patented layout uses four lugs on the push button and four on the rotating member (US 3,137,276, Weisser, filed 1955).
  - Pen Vibe's explanation of the Parker Jotter: the cam body turns 45 degrees as the button is pushed and another 45 degrees as it is released, giving two clicks per push.
  - Printed demos: print at 120-150% scale if the teeth are small, and grease the sliding faces (Printables 114490, Gamma4D); 0.15 mm layers, 2 perimeters, no supports, PLA, lubricate or sand the bevelled faces (Printables 695673).
  - Start with at least 0.3 mm clearance between moving printed parts; slopes print cleanly up to about 45-60 degrees overhang (Prusa Knowledge Base, Modeling with 3D printing in mind).
  - Do not use PLA for the spring: PLA 'makes for a very poor spring material' (Hackaday, 2026), and the signspinner maker used PETG for the printed spring because PLA would not survive being compressed.
  - Plastic springs held under constant deflection creep; use a metal spring for anything that stays compressed (DuPont General Design Principles, Module I, pp. 36-37).
  - Clicker switches are 'very inexpensive' and give tactile feedback, but are 'relatively mechanically complex, having a spring-loaded rotating ratcheting mechanism, and so tend to be less reliable than is desired' (US 7,880,100, Streamlight).

### When to use it

- **everyday_examples**: 
  - A retractable ballpoint or gel pen (the classic example; the Parker Jotter is the one Bill Hammack takes apart)
  - A flashlight with a clicky tail switch: US 4,827,385 describes its switch as 'of the rotating-ratcheting type used extensively in retractable ball point pens'
  - A maker's dishwasher 'clean/dirty' sign that turns a quarter turn on each push (Hackaday, 2019) *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **when_to_use**: Use it when one button, pushed the same way every time, should switch between two states: out and in, on and off, open and locked. It fits neatly inside a tube or round housing, so it suits pens, torches, retractable tools, pop-out buttons and hidden compartments. It is also a compact way to step something round by a fixed amount per push, such as a status dial, a counter or a rotary switch, without a motor.
- **when_not_to_use**: Avoid it for anything that carries real load or must never slip (use a ball-lock pin, index plunger or ratchet and pawl). Avoid it in a flat panel with little depth, where a heart-cam push-push latch or a slide latch fits better. Avoid it where the user must be able to see or set the state directly (a toggle switch or slide switch shows its position). Avoid it in dusty, gritty or sticky places, where grit in the small teeth makes it jam, and where very high reliability is needed (Streamlight's patent calls clicker switches less reliable than desired). If you need many positions rather than two, use a detent or ratchet indexer.
- **environment_limits**: Grit, lint, dried ink or glue in the teeth make it stick. Plastic return springs lose force over time if held compressed (DuPont, pp. 36-37), so use a metal spring. *(unverified parts omitted)*
- **safety_notes**: Low hazard. The spring can jump out when you take one apart. Small printed parts and springs are a choking hazard for young children.
- **common_failures**: Sticks half-way or will not turn: burrs, elephant's foot or too little clearance; clean and grease the sloped faces, sand lightly, or reprint with more clearance. Clicks but will not stay out: the tips of the ledges are rounded or worn, the spring is weak, or the parts are too small to print accurately; print larger and use a fresh metal spring. Turns but jams on the next push: the parts are not concentric or the lugs are too loose in the channels. Wears out after many clicks: small plastic teeth rub on every push; use PETG or nylon and grease, or buy a moulded mechanism. Printed spring goes weak: PLA springs creep; use PETG or a steel spring.
- **related**: 
  - Push-push latch (heart-cam latch): the flat version; a pin follows a heart-shaped track instead of a turning part stepping round a tube
  - Ratchet and pawl: also turns one way by one tooth at a time, but is driven round by a lever or handle and does not alternate between two held states
  - Geneva drive and other stepping mechanisms: turn continuous rotation (not a push) into steps
  - Bistable compliant mechanism (snap-through switch): two stable states from one springy printed part, but it is flipped back and forth rather than pushed the same way each time
  - Ball detent or compliant detent: clicks into set positions but you choose the direction; nothing makes it alternate
  - Sideways-shifting cam (US 2,865,331, Paper Mate, 1958): an early retractable-pen design where the follower moves sideways between two bores instead of turning
  - Twist-action pens: a helical cam turned by hand instead of pushed
